           ARRANGEMENT—PRESS RELEASE; STAFF REPORT; AND
           STATEMENT BY THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR FOR
           PAKISTAN
           In the context of the Third Review Under the Extended Arrangement Under the Extended
           Fund Facility and Second Review Under the Resilience and Sustainability Facility
           Arrangement, the following documents have been released and are included in this
           package:

           •   A Press Release including a statement by the Chair of the Executive Board.

           •   The Staff Report prepared by a staff team of the IMF for the Executive Board’s
               consideration on May 8, 2026, following discussions that ended on March 2, 2026,
               with the officials of Pakistan on economic developments and policies underpinning
               the IMF arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility. Based on information available
               at the time of these discussions, the staff report was completed on April 23, 2026.

           •   A World Bank Assessment Letter for the Resilience and Sustainability Facility

           •   A Staff Supplement updating information on recent developments.

           •   A Statement by the Executive Director for Pakistan.


           The IMF’s transparency policy allows for the deletion of market-sensitive information and
           premature disclosure of the authorities’ policy intentions in published staff reports and
           other documents.

                                                                                                  PR 26/147

IMF Executive Board Completes Third Review of the Extended
 Arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility and Second
    Review of the Arrangement under the Resilience and
            Sustainability Facility with Pakistan
                                 FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE


• The IMF Executive Board completed the third review of the Extended Arrangement under
  the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) and the second review of the arrangement under the
  Resilience and Sustainability Facility (RSF), allowing the authorities to draw the equivalent
  of about US$1.1 billion and about US$220 million, respectively. This brings total
  disbursements under the two arrangements to about $4.8 billion (SDR 3,348 billion).

• The authorities’ strong implementation, despite the Middle East war, has maintained
  economic stability and improved financing and external conditions. The shocks emanating
  from the Middle East war underline the continued importance of maintaining strong policies
  to continue building resilience and of moving ahead with structural reforms to achieve
  sustainable long-term growth.

• Policy priorities remain centered on maintaining macroeconomic stability and advancing
  reforms to strengthen public finances, enhance competition, raise productivity and
  competitiveness, bolster the social safety net and human capital, reform SOEs, and
  improve public service provision and energy sector viability.

Washington, DC – May 8, 2026: Today, the Executive Board of the International Monetary
Fund (IMF) completed the third review of Pakistan’s economic reform program supported by
the EFF and the second review of Pakistan’s RSF arrangement. This decision allows for an
immediate disbursement of around US$1.1 billion (SDR 760 million) under the EFF
arrangement and around US$220 million (SDR 154 million) under the RSF arrangement,
bringing total disbursements under the two arrangements to about $4.8 billion (SDR 3,348
billion).

Pakistan’s 37-month EFF arrangement was approved on September 25, 2024, and aims to
build resilience and enable sustainable growth. Key priorities include (i) entrenching
macroeconomic stability through consistent implementation of sound macro policies, including
rebuilding international reserve buffers and broadening the tax base; (ii) advancing reforms to
strengthen competition and raise productivity and competitiveness; and (iii) reforming SOEs
and improving public service provision, developing human and physical capital by expanding
health, education, and social protection spending, restoring energy sector viability, and
intensifying anti-corruption efforts.

Pakistan’s policy efforts under the EFF arrangement have delivered significant progress in
stabilizing the economy and rebuilding confidence amid a challenging global environment,
including the ongoing Middle East war. Fiscal performance has been strong, with a primary
surplus of 1.6 percent of GDP expected to be achieved in FY26, in line with targets. Inflation
has increased as higher global commodity prices have passed through to domestic energy
                                                  2


prices. Gross reserves stood at $16 billion at end-December, up from $14.5 billion at end-June
2025, and are projected to continue to be rebuilt in the next year and over the medium term.

The 28-month RSF arrangement was approved on May 9, 2025, and is supporting the
authorities’ efforts to reduce vulnerabilities to natural disasters and to build economic and
climate resilience. The authorities’ reforms prioritize: (i) building resilience to natural disasters
and strengthening public investment processes at all levels of government; (ii) making scarce
water resource usage more efficient, including through better pricing; (iii) strengthening
federal-provincial coordination of natural disaster response; (iv) improving the information
architecture for, and disclosure of, climate-related risks by banks and corporates; and
(v) supporting Pakistan’s efforts to meet its mitigation commitments and reduce related
macro-critical risks.

Following the Executive Board discussion, Mr. Nigel Clarke, Deputy Managing Director and
Acting Chair, made the following statement:

“Pakistan’s strong program implementation under the EFF arrangement has continued, which
has supported macroeconomic stability and the rebuilding of fiscal and foreign exchange
buffers. GDP growth accelerated, inflation remained contained, and the current account was
broadly balanced in the first nine months of FY26. Amid a more challenging and highly
uncertain external environment since the onset of the war in the Middle East, Pakistan needs
to maintain strong macroeconomic policies while accelerating reform efforts, which are critical
to managing further shocks and fostering higher sustainable medium-term growth.

“The authorities’ commitment to the FY26 and FY27 primary balance targets will help
strengthen fiscal sustainability and policy credibility. Gradual fiscal consolidation remains
appropriate to strengthen resilience and should be supported by continued efforts to boost
revenue mobilization—through broadening the tax net and improving compliance—and
strengthen spending efficiency and public financial management. These efforts would also
create space for scaling up social assistance, human capital development, and productive
public investment, while addressing tax policy distortions.

“The State Bank of Pakistan has acted proactively to maintain an appropriately tight monetary
policy stance aimed at keeping inflation expectations anchored and should continue to
carefully monitor potential second-round effects on domestic prices, wages, and expectations.
Exchange rate flexibility should be the main shock absorber, particularly given the need to
continue rebuilding reserves. Efforts to deepen the FX market should continue, including
through a carefully-sequenced medium-term FX liberalization. Proactive efforts to support
financial stability remain important, including ensuring all banks continue to be adequately
capitalized, and addressing microfinance banks’ capital shortfalls.

“In an environment of high and volatile commodity prices, recent improvements in energy
sector finances need to be sustained by keeping domestic fuel, electricity, and gas prices in
line with costs, while protecting the most vulnerable consumers with targeted support.
Continued reform efforts to reduce costs and address inefficiencies will safeguard the sector’s
viability and improve Pakistan’s competitiveness.

“Advancing and deepening structural reforms is essential to generate sustainable economic
growth and attract high-impact private investment. Efforts should continue to deliver on the
Economic Governance Reform actions aimed at bolstering anti-corruption institutions. Other
priorities include completing SOE reforms and privatization and enhancing the business
environment by eliminating distortions and unnecessary regulations.
                                              3


“Reducing Pakistan’s vulnerability to climate shocks will enhance macroeconomic and fiscal
sustainability. Reforms supported by the RSF are helping to strengthen natural disaster
response and financing coordination, improve the use of scarce water resources, reflect
climate considerations in project selection and budgeting, and improve the climate information
architecture.”

                 PAKISTAN
                 THIRD REVIEW UNDER THE EXTENDED ARRANGEMENT
April 23, 2026
                 UNDER THE EXTENDED FUND FACILITY AND SECOND
                 REVIEW UNDER THE RESILIENCE AND SUSTAINABILITY
                 FACILITY ARRANGEMENT

                 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
                 Recent Developments. Strong policy implementation has continued to support
                 Pakistan’s economic recovery, build confidence, and bolster its resilience to shocks. GDP
                 growth accelerated in FY26H1, while inflation remained contained, the current account
                 was broadly balanced, and reserve rebuilding exceeded earlier projections. However,
                 the impact of the war in the Middle East clouds Pakistan’s near-term outlook and there
                 is great uncertainty about how developments will unfold. Under the baseline scenario,
                 the war is expected to put upward pressure on inflation and weigh on growth and the
                 balance of payments, but the overall impact is expected to be contained. However,
                 downside risks are high.

                 Program Performance. The 37-month Extended Arrangement under the Extended
                 Fund Facility (EFF), approved on September 25, 2024, is on track, underpinned by strong
                 performance. All seven QPCs and six of eight ITs were met at end-December. Most
                 continuous and other SBs were met. Completion of the third EFF review would make
                 available SDR 760 million (about US$1.1 billion), bringing total disbursements under the
                 EFF to SDR 3,040 million. The 29-month Resilience and Sustainability Facility (RSF)
                 arrangement, approved on May 9, 2025, is also on track. Both RMs due for this (second)
                 RSF review (RMs 7 and 8) have been met. Completion of the second RSF review would
                 make available SDR 153.8 million (about US$220 million).

                 EFF and RSF Policies. Amidst a very uncertain international environment and against
                 domestic concerns about the still elevated poverty rate and subdued growth, strong
                 policy and steadfast reform efforts remain paramount to safeguarding economic
                 stability and supporting sustained higher growth. The authorities remain committed to
                 program policies and objectives, including: (i) the gradual fiscal consolidation plan and
                 accelerating efforts to broaden the tax base to generate resources needed for higher
                 social and development spending, alongside reforms to improve spending efficiency
PAKISTAN




and public financial management; (ii) an appropriately tight monetary policy to ensure inflation
expectations remain anchored in the face of volatile commodity prices;
(iii) maintaining a flexible exchange rate as the key shock absorber to support the rebuilding of
reserves; (iv) keeping fuel and energy tariffs in line with cost recovery to avoid unaffordable
subsidies and fiscal costs, while protecting vulnerable consumers with targeted support and
continuing to implement reforms to address the high costs of energy; and (v) advancing and
deepening structural reforms to sustainably boost long-term growth, including delivering on
the Economic Governance Reform actions and strengthening anti-corruption institutions,
advancing SOE reforms and the privatization agenda, and eliminating distortions and
unnecessary regulations. Policies adopted under the RSF arrangement will reduce economic
vulnerabilities from climate shocks, improve the climate information architecture, and help
facilitate climate-focused investment.

RECENT DEVELOPMENTS
1.     Pakistan’s recovery gained momentum in the first eight months of FY26, but the
impact from the Middle East war weighs on the near-term outlook (Box 1):

•       Economic activity and inflation. Despite the floods in July–August, GDP growth picked up in
        FY26 H1, averaging 3.8 percent (yoy), driven by the auto, construction, and garment industries.
        High-frequency indicators remained robust in January–February 2026. Headline inflation
        increased to 7.3 percent (yoy) in March, as higher commodity prices began passing through to
        domestic energy prices. Core inflation (7.6 percent (yoy) in March) has so far remained contained,
        after stabilizing at around 7½ since early FY26.

•       Monetary policy. Following the stabilization in inflation, the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC)
        cut the policy rate by 50 basis points in December and held it at 10.5 percent in January and
        March. The State Bank of Pakistan (SBP) lowered the cash reserve requirement (CRR) for banks
        from 6 to 5 percent effective January 30, to promote greater private sector lending. Lending to
        the private sector has started to recover—albeit from a low base—posting growth of 15 percent
        (yoy) in February 2026.
•       External conditions and the FX market. The current account was broadly balanced over the
        first nine months of FY26. Remittance inflows remained robust, partially offsetting a wider trade
        deficit due to higher import demand and slower export growth. Gross reserves continued to
        exceed program targets, reaching $16 billion at end-December 2025, benefiting from SBP FX
        purchases, and the rupee has remained broadly stable, at around PRs 280 per dollar. After the
        Middle East war started, Pakistan faced some capital outflows and sovereign spreads temporarily
        widened to around 500bps. However, spreads returned to near pre-war levels by mid-April, and

        Pakistan returned to the Eurobond market for the first time since 2022 with a private placement
        of a $750 million, 3-year bond.

        targets were met. The overall balance improved by 1.7 percent of GDP, helped by lower interest
        payments, and public debt has continued to gradually decline.

•       Power sector. The end-December 2025 circular debt (CD) flow target was met, supported by
        strong collection performance in the context of improved DISCO management and enforcement.

6        INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                                                PAKISTAN


        Industrial power consumption remained very strong through February 2026 (averaging
        25 percent yoy), driven by the ongoing captive power plant (CPP) transition to the grid and,
        likely, stronger underlying industrial activity. More recently, other sectors also saw a jump in
        consumption, with overall consumption growth reaching 9 percent (yoy) in December-February.

•       Gas sector. Regular tariff adjustments in line with costs have prevented additional accumulation
        of principal CD. However, late payment surcharges on the large existing stock caused further
        overall CD accumulation, to PRs 3.4 trillion (2.7 percent of GDP) as of December 2025. Gas
        companies’ financial health has been challenged by weak domestic consumption, lower power
        sector demand, and the CPP transition despite industrial consumption growth of 29 percent in
        H1 FY27 (yoy).

PROGRAM PERFORMANCE
2.            Program implementation has remained in line with commitments (MEFP Tables 1–2):

•       Performance Criteria (PCs). The authorities met all seven quantitative PCs for end-December
        2025: the floors on (i) the net international reserves of the SBP; (ii) the number of new tax returns;
        and (iii) targeted cash transfer spending (BISP); and the ceilings on (iv) the net domestic assets of
        the SBP; (v) the SBP’s stock of net foreign currency swaps/forward position; (vi) the general
        government primary budget deficit; and (vii) the amount of government guarantees. They also
        met both continuous PCs on (i) zero new flow of SBP credit to the government; and (ii) zero
        external public payment arrears.

•       Indicative Targets (ITs). Six of eight end-December 2025 ITs were met, including: the floors on
        (i) the weighted average time-to-maturity of the local currency domestic debt securities stock;
        (ii) general government health and education spending; (iii) consolidated net tax revenues
        collected by provincial revenue authorities; and the ceilings on (iv) the aggregate provincial


                                                                                                       INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND    7
PAKISTAN


        primary budget deficit; (v) net accumulation of tax refund arrears; and (vi) power sector payment
        arrears. However, end-December ITs were missed for the floors on: (i) net tax revenues collected
        by the FBR; and (ii) tax revenues collected by the FBR from retailers.

•       Structural Benchmarks (SBs). Five of six SBs were met, including on the publication of a
        governance action plan based on the findings of the Governance Diagnostic Assessment; the
        implementation of priority actions (covering taxpayer audits, digital invoicing, and production
        monitoring) from the FBR transformation plan; preparation of a phaseout plan for fiscal
        incentives to Special Technology Zones; implementation of the annual inflation-adjustment of
        the UCT (Kafaalat) program; and the notification of the semi-annual gas tariff adjustment.
        The end-March 2026 SB on amendments to the SWF Act to adopt governance mechanisms and
        safeguards following international standards was not met, but the amendments are pending
        Cabinet approval. Two of three continuous SBs were met, but the continuous SB on not granting
        preferential tax treatments was missed due to an extension of the tax exemption for sugar
        imports, which was subsequently repealed without any utilization.

•       Reform Measures (RMs). Both RMs due under the second RSF review – on (i) the issuance of
        SBP guidelines for management of climate-related financial risks (RM7); and (ii) issuance of SECP
        guidelines for disclosure of climate-related risks and opportunities (RM8) – were met.


OUTLOOK AND RISKS
3.      The updated macroeconomic projections reflect the more challenging outlook as a
result of the Middle East war, which is expected to push up inflation and weigh on growth and
the BOP (Box 1, Tables 1–7):

8        INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                                             PAKISTAN



                                            Box 1. Impact of the Middle East War
Pakistan’s economy is highly exposed to spillovers from the war in the Middle East:
•      Energy imports. As a net oil and gas importer, Pakistan is heavily reliant on GCC supplies, with
       81 percent of fuel imports coming from the region, and is being impacted by both higher international
       prices and the rise in regional price premia over Brent/WTI, particularly for refined products. In the event
       of sustained disruptions to the physical availability of fuel imports, impacts on economic activity would
       likely be even larger than implied by the increase in international prices.
•      Food and fertilizer imports. Immediate exposures to fertilizer trade disruptions appear manageable, as
       Pakistan has been largely self-sufficient in urea production in recent years, but a prolonged disruption to
       DAP supply chains could potentially impact the Kharif planting season in June-July. Food import prices
       could also be impacted in the event of prolonged fertilizer trade disruptions.
•      Remittances. Pakistan receives annual remittances amounting to about 9 percent of GDP, of which
       55 percent come from the GCC. A significant disruption to the GCC economies and/or return of migrant
       workers could weigh on these flows, a major source of financing for consumption and the BOP.
•      Capital flows. The deterioration in global financial conditions has already resulted in capital outflows,
       which are likely to continue to intensify if the crisis extends. Access to short-term commercial financing,
       which is largely from GCC banks, could also be impacted if risk sentiment deteriorates significantly.

The authorities responded to the crisis by temporarily delaying fuel price increases to develop a
targeted subsidy scheme. After passing through an initial 20 percent price increase on March 7, the
government delayed further fuel price rises by providing a temporary subsidy to oil marketing companies at
a cost of 0.1 percent of GDP, funded by a package of saving measures (MEFP ¶6). This subsidy was unwound
on April 3 (though a temporary 80 Rs/L reduction in PDL on diesel remained), resulting in initial increases of
18 and 55 percent for gasoline and diesel prices, respectively. The authorities also shifted to weekly
adjustments of gasoline and diesel prices. At the same time, the federal and provincial governments
announced a targeted relief package for vulnerable groups impacted by the price rise, including (limited)
transfers for motorbike owners and small farmers, and subsidized public transport in Punjab and Islamabad.
All relief measures are expected to be budget neutral. The government has also announced temporary fuel
saving measures, including a 4-day work week for the public sector and mandatory remote work for
50 percent of public and private sector employees.



                                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND                     9
PAKISTAN


                              Box 1. Impact of the Middle East War (concluded)
    Under the updated baseline scenario, which follows the April 2026 WEO reference scenario
    assumptions for the duration of the war, the impact on Pakistan’s outlook is expected to be
    moderate:
    •    GDP growth is projected to slow by 0.2 percent in FY26 and 0.6 percent in FY27, due to the drag on
         external and domestic demand.
    •    CPI Inflation (average) is pushed up by about ½ pp in FY26 and 1½pp in FY27, mostly though pass-
         through to consumer energy prices, with more modest impacts expected on food and core inflation.
    •    The current account is projected to worsen by around 0.2pp in FY26 and 0.4pp in FY27, as higher fuel
         imports are expected to be partially offset by compression of non-oil imports, particularly in FY27.
    •    With fiscal space very limited, the authorities plan to offset the cost of absorbing the delayed
         adjustment of fuel prices and targeted relief to households by constraining primary expenditures.
    The outlook would deteriorate further under an adverse scenario. Under the April 2026 WEO adverse
    scenario, the cumulative hit to GDP would rise to around 1½ pp by FY27, while the FY27 projected impact
    for inflation and the current account deficit would increase by around 2½ pp and 1½ percent of GDP,
    respectively, relative to a pre-conflict baseline.
    Sound policies should anchor the response to any adverse scenario to safeguard economic and
    financial stability. With government revenues impacted by lower domestic demand and non-oil imports,
    further expenditure compression and reprioritization would be needed to remain within the program’s fiscal
    envelope and ensure debt sustainability while providing temporary and targeted transfers to protect the
    most vulnerable. The exchange rate should remain the primary shock absorber, while the SBP should
    maintain appropriately tight monetary policy to prevent deanchoring of inflation expectations.


•       GDP and Inflation. GDP growth is projected to reach 3.6 percent in FY26, reflecting strong
        activity through February (¶3). However, growth in FY27 is revised down due to higher
        commodity prices and weak external demand resulting from the Middle East conflict. Amid
        higher commodity prices, CPI inflation is expected to exceed 10 percent in Q4, and to reach
        8.4 percent in FY27, before returning to the SBP target range in FY28.

•       Balance of Payments. The current account is projected to record a small deficit in FY26, with an
        acceleration in import growth over the remaining months due to the spike in global energy
        prices. The current account is expected to further deteriorate in FY27 on account of elevated oil
        and gas prices, although lower domestic demand is expected to mitigate the impact on the trade
        deficit from energy imports. Over the medium-term, the current account deficit is expected to
        remain around 1 percent of GDP, reflecting an expected normalization of non-oil import demand
        and tariff cuts under the National Tariff Policy (NTP). Remittances are expected to remain an
        important source of inflows. Access to market financing is expected to continue following the
        recent market re-entry.

•       Public Debt. Under the baseline, public debt remains sustainable over the medium-term (Annex
        I). Medium-term risks remain high, reflecting Pakistan’s large gross financing needs and
        challenges in obtaining external financing (including high-quality foreign investment).

4.     The outlook is clouded by significant risks. Key downside risks include geopolitical
tensions and intensification of conflicts (including a more extended war in the Middle East),
commodity price volatility, tightening in global financial conditions (including higher long-term

10      INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                    PAKISTAN


interest rates and rising risk premia), lower remittances due to economic disruptions or tighter
immigration policies in key destination countries, and escalating global protectionism and trade
disruptions. The risk of policy, including fiscal, slippages also remains high. There are also upside
risks for a faster recovery benefiting from the consistent implementation of policies and
strengthening confidence, and from reaping economic dividends from Pakistan’s enhanced relations
with bilateral partners.


EFF POLICY DISCUSSIONS
Policy discussions focused on the implementation of the gradual fiscal consolidation plans in FY26
and FY27, with due support to vulnerable households, and the entrenchment of monetary and
financial stability in the context of a significant supply shock and high uncertainty about the
global environment in the months ahead. Discussions also focused on the need to accelerate the
reform momentum to support stronger growth by improving governance, domestic competition
and the business environment, reducing the state footprint, strengthening the energy sector, and
boosting climate resilience.

A. Fiscal Policy

5.      The authorities remain committed to the FY26 underlying primary balance target of
1.6 percent of GDP. To mitigate the projected FBR collection shortfall and ensure that the primary
balance target is achieved mostly through revenue mobilization efforts, as a prior action, the
authorities plan to collect overdue tax revenue resulting from recent court rulings in favor of FBR
(0.3 percent of GDP, largely stemming from “super tax”).1 They will also revise FY26 expenditure
ceilings (0.1 percent of GDP) related to unutilized emergency response allocations. As subsidies
seeking to prevent domestic fuel price adjustment have proven distortionary and fiscally
unsustainable, any fiscal policy response to high fuel prices should be targeted, limited, temporary,
and budget neutral (MEFP ¶5-6). To this end, the authorities plan to align domestic fuel prices with
international prices as a prior action, while provincial authorities have introduced targeted support
to vulnerable households (Box 1).

6.      To ensure a sustainable medium-term fiscal position, the FY27 budget (end-June 2026
SB) will remain in line with the program target of 2 percent of GDP (MEFP ¶7). This is
appropriate given Pakistan’s limited fiscal space and the need to continue to reduce public debt to
more sustainable levels, amid recurrent shocks to the global economy. Achieving the FY27 target
requires additional revenue collection measures of 0.6 percent of GDP (Box 2) to address Pakistan’s
low tax buoyancy and ensure that the fiscal effort is achieved through sustained growth of the tax
revenue ratio. At the federal level, revenue mobilization will focus on gains from the implementation
of the FBR’s transformation plan and streamlining tax expenditures (0.3 percent of GDP in revenues
in FY27) in line with recent IMF CD recommendations. In this regard, an FBR revenue collection floor
is proposed to be set as a QPC starting in December 2026. At the provincial level, revenue
mobilization will continue to focus on broadening the GST tax base on services and the application

1
    A tax on corporate taxpayers and high earners originally announced in the FY23 budget.


                                                                             INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND   11
PAKISTAN


of higher income tax rates on agricultural income. Expenditure restraint will also be essential, with
primary spending remaining flat as a share of GDP in FY27, while increasing targeted cash transfers
and health and education spending ratios (¶14-15). Further expenditure compression (including
lower-priority capital spending) may be needed if risks to revenue mobilization materialize. Amid
exceptionally high budget uncertainty, setting aside an adequate contingency reserve would be
critical to buffer fiscal risks, including those related to assumptions about the impact of the war in
the Middle East. Any potential interest-bill savings should also be set aside to safeguard the gradual
consolidation path and build fiscal buffers.

7.       Persistent shortfalls in FBR tax revenues underscore the risks of a narrow tax base and
call for base broadening via revenue administration reforms. The FBR has implemented priority
areas of its transformation plan (end-March 2026 SB) including: (i) strengthening taxpayer audits by
deploying the Compliance Risk Management (CRM) system, (ii) expanding and eventually making
mandatory the use of digital invoicing, and (iii) enhancing production monitoring (MEFP ¶9). To
ensure even-handed treatment of taxpayers, the FBR will prepare an audit manual and audit policy
centralizing the audit case selection process via administrative prioritization and monitoring of high-
risk cases through the CRM system (new proposed end-August 2026 SB, MEFP ¶10). Revenue gains
from these interventions are likely to materialize in FY27, but they continue to target primarily
existing taxpayers. Revenue administration efforts need to shift toward intensifying the retailer tax
registration scheme and empowering the FBR to restrict specified high value transactions to
individuals who have filed an income tax return.

8.      Work on a medium-term tax reform strategy is ongoing. The newly created Tax Policy
Office (TPO) should be fully staffed by end-May 2025. A data sharing arrangement governing the
relationship between the TPO and FBR has been signed and the office has reached out to relevant
stakeholders soliciting tax reform proposals. However, any tax reform proposals must be carefully
calibrated, tested, and communicated to ensure long-term tax policy stability and consistency with
the overall fiscal objectives (MEFP ¶11).

9.       Expanding the Treasury Single Account (TSA) is a key priority of public financial
management reforms. As a first step, the authorities are adding 70 new accounts to 149 accounts
already covered in the sweeping arrangement, roughly doubling the balances available to the TSA.
There is scope to further broaden the inclusion criteria, which would help improve the government’s
liquidity position and reduce borrowing costs.




12   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              PAKISTAN


                                                                                    Box 2. Potential for Further Revenue Mobilization
Although thanks to the authorities’ efforts, tax revenues to GDP in Pakistan have reached their
highest level since at least 2000, they remain considerably below those of peer countries. The increase
since 2022 has been higher than a typical 3-year increase in peer countries, however, the tax revenue ratio
(including provincial taxes and the PDL) only reached 12.3 percent of GDP in FY2025, still below the 25 th
percentile of peer countries. Given Pakistan’s large social and development needs as well as high public debt,
the revenue mobilization effort needs to be sustained.

Pakistan’s tax base remains concentrated in a few sectors. An analysis based on input-output tables
(2007-2022) suggests that agriculture is the single largest undertaxed sector. Despite contributing 24.6
percent of value added, the effective tax rate of the agricultural sector is just 0.3 percent. Agricultural income
is taxed by the provinces. In 2025, agricultural income tax rates were increased significantly to align with tax
rates for other income; however, revenues remained below expectations reflecting implementation delays
and enforcement challenges. Textiles, real estate, and business services also account for a large share of
value added compared to net tax collection. At the other extreme, petroleum products carry an effective tax
rate of 166 percent, leaving revenues heavily reliant on fuel taxation and vulnerable to shocks.

GST efficiency is low. The GST C-efficiency ratio1 has declined from 27.4 percent to 22.8 percent over the
past ten years. Pakistan’s 18 percent standard GST rate is not low by regional standards; however, barely
one-quarter of the theoretical base is taxed. A broad set of basic goods remains exempt or concessionally
taxed, historical zero-rating in export sectors has narrowed the base, and post-devolution fragmentation of
GST on services has added compliance and administrative complexity through four separate provincial
regimes. Raising C-efficiency to 35 percent (still well below peers such as Turkey, Morocco, and Indonesia)
would imply roughly Rs 2.1 trillion in additional GST revenue (1.8 percent of GDP) on a FY2024-25 base.
The authorities can advance tax revenue mobilization by (i) eliminating sales tax expenditures,
(ii) improving compliance, and (iii) increasing provincial revenues. FBR’s 2025 Tax Expenditure report
estimates that around 1.2 percent of GDP in various tax exemptions remain in place (notably on sales of
residential property and certain foods). Sales tax compliance could be increased through production
monitoring, adoption of digital invoicing and FBR retailer registration. Provinces should accelerate the
collection of GST on services by strengthening enforcement. To ensure that gains from the agricultural
income tax (AIT) reform materialize, provinces should take advantage of data sharing with the FBR, increase
the automation of AIT procedures and dedicate additional human and IT resources to enforcement. IMF CD
has been providing regular tailored advice to provinces to address their specific implementation challenges.
1/ The C-efficiency ratio, defined as actual GST revenue divided by the product of the standard rate and private final
consumption, captures the combined effect of exemptions, zero-rating, compliance gaps, and base erosion.


                                                                                                                                                                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND                     13
PAKISTAN


10.       Progress in strengthening debt management is continuing. Efforts to extend domestic
maturities are ongoing and the average time to maturity (ATM) of the local currency domestic debt
stock increased to around 4 years (end-December 2025), up from 2.7 years at the program’s outset.
Transparency regarding provincial participation in T-bill auctions has improved. Analysis is underway
to develop reform options for the retail financing program, including through use of digital
technologies. With investor base diversification a priority, especially given the significant sovereign-
bank nexus, the authorities are advancing their work to publish an action plan for local currency
bond market development (end-September 2026 SB). The new fiscal risk monitoring framework,
published in December 2025, establishes a consistent approach for monitoring and reporting
contingent liabilities in PPP projects. Regular updates of the summary of outstanding contingent
liabilities will enhance accountability and risk management (MEFP ¶16). Amid heightened uncertainty
in the coming period, market communication efforts should be strengthened to clearly articulate the
authorities’ debt management strategy, while fully respecting market-determined interest rates.

11.     The authorities are committed to greater transparency and competition in public
procurement. Application of the digitalized procurement system (e-PADS) continues to be
expanded, including through the roll-out of an upgraded system and its application to federal and
provincial agencies (MEFP ¶15). The authorities will also encourage SOEs to develop procurement
rules that would help incorporate SOE procurement in e-PADs. Finally, the adoption of recently
drafted legal amendments of the relevant provisions of the Public Procurement Regulatory Authority
(PPRA) rules will eliminate preferences for SOEs in awarding public procurement contracts without
competition, subject to limited and reasonable exceptions (new proposed end-September 2026
SB). An external audit of the e-PADS system has been completed by the Auditor General and is
expected to be approved soon.

B. Poverty Reduction and Social Protection

12.      Strengthening social protection and human capital investment is critical to create
sustained gains in poverty reduction, employment, productivity, and long-term GDP growth.
Pakistan’s poverty headcount rate increased to 25.3 percent in FY24, up from 18.3 percent in FY22,
exacerbated by overlapping crises (COVID, floods, economic instability and inflation).2 Health and
education outcomes remain very weak compared with lower middle-income countries.3 A strong
social safety net and more effective human capital investment are a prerequisite to building a more
sustainable and productive growth path for Pakistan, and to buffering Pakistan’s most vulnerable
from shocks, including commodity price shocks. A dollar of cash transfers generates a real income
multiplier of 2.37 in Pakistan, likely through greater demand and employment creation for low-
income Pakistanis.4

14.    The authorities will also target greater health and education spending in FY27. The end-
December target on aggregate health and education spending was comfortably met, although some
provinces missed individual targets. The 0.3 percent of GDP increase in health and education
spending for FY26 (to 2.8 percent of GDP) appears on track to be fully executed. A similarly
ambitious increase of 0.2 percent of GDP (to 3.0 percent of GDP) is targeted for FY27.

C. Monetary, Exchange Rate, and Financial Sector

15.      The SBP’s monetary policy has helped contain inflation and inflation expectations, but
in view of potential price pressures from the ongoing energy and food shock, the SBP would
need to continue maintaining an appropriately tight stance. Benign inflation developments in
FY26H1 gave the SBP room to cut the policy rate to 10.5 percent in December, before prudently
holding rates flat in early March following the outbreak of the Middle East war. However, with
inflation projected to increase on the back of higher international commodity prices, the SBP should
carefully monitor and assess the pass-through to domestic prices, wages, and expectations and stand
ready to tighten policy as needed to ensure inflation remains anchored around its inflation objective.
At the same time, the SBP should remain attentive to the recent momentum in private credit, as
further acceleration could add to demand pressures. Against the current challenging external
backdrop, the SBP’s ongoing review of MPC communication (MEFP ¶23) presents an important
opportunity to further improve the market’s understanding of the monetary policy reaction
function—including how the SBP deploys and sequences its instruments—and enhances the
credibility of the inflation target.




                                                                              INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND             15
PAKISTAN


16.       The exchange rate should remain the key shock absorber, while longer-term reforms to
deepen and liberalize the FX market should continue. While the SBP’s proactive approach to
reserve accumulation has helped increase foreign exchange buffers, reserve cover remains too low
by standard reserve metrics, and certainly inadequate to support any specific exchange rate. With the
economy now facing the largest external shock since 2022-23, exchange rate flexibility remains the
first line of defense should exchange pressures emerge, to allow the interbank market to balance
supply and demand efficiently. Efforts to deepen the FX market should continue, including by
improving the infrastructure for cross-border payments based on the SBP’s comprehensive
assessment of remittance costs (end-May 2026 SB). Over the medium term, careful liberalization of
the foreign exchange market could support a deeper FX market, attract new inflows, and ease the
operating environment for domestic firms. To this end, the SBP has committed to preparing a
roadmap for gradual, phased and conditions-based liberalization of the foreign exchange market
(new proposed end-March 2027 SB). Furthermore, the authorities should unwind the January 2022
shortening of the repatriation period for export proceeds (assessed as an outflow CFM under the IMF
Institutional View on the Liberalization and Management of Capital Flows) once macroeconomic and
balance of payments stability is restored.

17.     Continued enforcement and monitoring of the regulatory framework is critical to
maintaining financial stability. As of end-December 2025, the previously undercapitalized private
bank has met applicable capital requirements. Close supervisory oversight should ensure the
maintenance of adequate capital buffers against potential adverse developments. Overall, the
banking sector remains liquid and profitable, although this is largely driven by significant exposures
to government securities, which also pose risks to financial stability and call for advancing bond
market development and investor base diversification. Undercapitalization continues to be a
challenge for microfinance banks, with four of eleven banks remaining undercapitalized, driving the
sector’s overall negative capital adequacy ratio. The SBP is committed to addressing the capital
shortfall and should take decisive actions to review microfinance banks’ business models
(MEFP ¶27a).

18.      Clear guidance on the financial system’s structure post-2027 will help align market
expectations and support financial stability. The authorities’ financial sector strategy will provide a
detailed roadmap for the transition to a constitutionally mandated “riba” (interest) free economy
(end-June 2026 SB). In addition to outlining the legal, regulatory, and strategic implications for
monetary policy, public debt and financial management, and bank and nonbank financial institution
supervision, the strategy should establish, without ambiguity, the implementation trajectory for
financial institutions and the approach to addressing outstanding conventional liabilities. The Virtual
Assets Bill approved by Parliament in March 2026 formally established the Pakistan Virtual Assets
Regulatory Authority (PVARA). The authorities have committed to addressing any remaining gaps
relative to international standards through comprehensive secondary regulations. In general, efforts
to integrate virtual assets should align with broader macro-financial policies (MEFP ¶28).

19.    The authorities continue to strengthen the effectiveness of the AML/CFT framework
and address trade-based money laundering (TBML). Informed by the ongoing update of the 2023
National Risk Assessment, the National AML/CFT Authority is coordinating and prioritizing reforms of


16   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                            PAKISTAN


the AML/CFT framework in several key areas, including: (i) enhancing the effectiveness of AML/CFT
supervision of, and application of preventive measures by, designated non‑financial businesses and
professions (DNFBPs); (ii) improving the availability and accuracy of beneficial ownership (BO)
information, particularly in SECP’s central registry; and (iii) strengthening efforts to combat TBML,
including developing a price verification database. Greater inter‑agency data sharing and use of
policy instruments remain areas of further improvement, including to more closely coordinate
foreign exchange reporting of import payments and customs data to better monitor and mitigate
macro- and transaction-level TBML risks.

D. Energy Sector

20.     Amid significant fuel price volatility, the authorities reaffirmed their commitment to
ensuring energy sector viability. The authorities have made significant progress in recent years in
aligning domestic energy prices with cost recovery, with regular automatic tariff adjustments. To
prevent any distortions in domestic demand patterns and safeguard cost recovery, the authorities
have committed to aligning domestic fuel prices with international markets at each fortnightly
adjustment period while also keeping power and gas tariffs in line with costs (see Box 3).5 In parallel,
progress is continuing on structural reforms to improve efficiency and reliability and reduce cost
pressures.

                                        Box 3. Energy Pricing in Pakistan
    Global energy prices pass through to local energy prices in a number of ways:
    •   Fuel prices: Ex-refinery petroleum and diesel prices are benchmarked against global prices and
        converted to rupees. Additional (relatively small) charges are added to account for import and inland
        freight costs, marketing company margins, and dealer commissions. A federal petroleum development
        levy is then added to arrive at the final cost.
    •   Electricity prices: During its annual rebasing process (recently shifted from July to January), NEPRA
        determines an overall sectoral revenue requirement and associated uniform weighted average tariff
        consistent with cost recovery based on DISCOs’ cost forecasts and its own assumptions, including the
        global crude oil price. The government then notifies a schedule of base tariffs per consumer category,
        including a tariff differential subsidy and policy choices regarding consumer categories (e.g., lower tariffs
        for protected consumers). To the notified base tariffs are added (1) quarterly tariff adjustments (QTAs) to
        fill gaps between projected capacity charges at the time of rebasing and actual charges; (2) monthly fuel
        charge adjustments (FCAs) to fill gaps between projected generation input costs (including global crude
        oil prices) at the time of rebasing and actual charges; (3) a debt service surcharge (DSS, which finances
        power sector debt payments); and (4) federal taxes. QTAs, FCAs, and DSS are not applied to the “lifeline”
        most-vulnerable consumers’ bills.

    •   Gas prices: OGRA carries out tariff adjustments in January and June of each year. OGRA determines an
        overall sectoral revenue requirement and associated average tariff consistent with cost recovery based
        on Suis’ inputs and its own assumptions, including the cost of RLNG. The government then notifies a
        schedule of base tariffs per consumer category, which includes progressively lower tariffs for protected
        consumers. Unlike the power sector, there are neither intra-year adjustments nor subsidies applied,
        although OGRA is directed to apply any prior-year surpluses to tariffs to finance CD stock reduction.



5
    The authorities shifted to weekly adjustments in March 2026.


                                                                             INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND           17
PAKISTAN


21.    Power DISCO operations have continued to improve, and some progress has been
made on the power sector structural reform agenda, but vulnerable consumers must remain
protected and non-operational sources of CD flow need to be tackled.

•      Tariff adjustment. Continued timely intra-year tariff adjustments have kept the overall power
       tariff structure in line with costs, and it is critical that these adjustments continue in the context
       of global fuel price shocks, including to avoid a resurgence in CD flow (see below).6 A large tariff
       reduction for industrial consumers in February maintained system cost-recovery by unwinding
       industrial consumers’ cross-subsidy to residential consumers, which was balanced out by
       increasing or introducing fixed charges on residential consumers, including some protected
       consumers. Any further tariff adjustments should preserve the progressive nature of the power
       tariff structure, alongside greater progress on power subsidy reform (¶26), to enable better
       targeting of vulnerable electricity consumers.

•      CD flow. The FY27 CD flow target has been set at PRs 300 billion, PRs 100 billion lower than
       FY26, reflecting continued improvement in operational performance. However, non-operational
       CD flow pressures remain (¶3), with greater progress needed on the delayed settlement of
       penalty payment arrears with remaining IPPs as part of the FY25-26 CD stock reduction plan.
       Lower expected CD flow will allow for a lower planned FY27 power subsidy, from 0.7 percent of
       GDP to 0.6 percent of GDP.

•      Advancing power sector reforms. The authorities remain committed to full implementation of
       the power structural reform agenda (MEFP ¶33) to reduce costs for residents and businesses in a
       sustainable fashion. The DISCO private sector participation process, which will lead to further
       operational improvements, is proceeding, albeit with a delay. Furthermore, a second wave of

    DISCOs is also being launched in parallel (preconditions for which are an end-December 2026
    SB). The transmission network restructuring has been finalized and the first wholesale electricity
    auctions should start in mid-2026. NEPRA’s recently introduced reform to shift solar consumers
    from a net metering to a net billing model, more in line with international practice, would allow
    for a greater balance between solar and grid consumption.7 However, the exemption of existing
    solar consumers from the new model retains a significant, and likely regressive, near-term cross
    subsidy from electricity grid to solar consumers.
22.    The global commodity shock will complicate efforts to address vulnerabilities in the
gas sector.

•   Disrupted LNG shipments from the Gulf have shifted the authorities’ attention to ensuring
    sufficient supply in the near term. Nonetheless, the RLNG surplus resulting from long-term
    contracts and falling demand remains to be addressed, and efforts to coordinate among relevant
    government stakeholders on a solution should continue. To this end, the recent establishment of
    a National Integrated Energy Plan secretariat, to be led by the Petroleum and Power Ministries, is
    a positive step toward better coordination of supply and demand across the energy sector.

•   The authorities are focused on reducing the large and growing stock of CD (¶3). To this end, they
    continue with timely tariff adjustments to recover costs and maintain a progressive tariff
    structure. The recent finalization of an audited and reliable data set of gas CD, and its quarterly
    dissemination to stakeholders, is a welcome

24.    The authorities are advancing their governance and anti-corruption agenda, seeking to
strengthen the integrity of policymaking:

•    Implementing the recommendations of the Governance and Corruption Diagnostic (GCD)
     (MEFP ¶38.a). The authorities published the Prime Minister’s Economic Governance Reform
     (EGR) Plan based upon the key GCD recommendations (end-December 2025 SB). The EGR sets
     out 15 reform actions with key performance indicators (KPIs), timelines, and monitoring
     modalities. The authorities have also established an institutional framework to facilitate
     implementation and have initiated mechanisms for close coordination with key stakeholders
     (including Civil Society Organizations) through policy dialogue and publication of semi-annual
     reports. To ensure high-level of transparency and accountability and better track EGR’s results
     and impact, the authorities have committed to further develop and publish outcome-based
     indicators in consultation with key stakeholders.

•    Strengthening the effectiveness of anti-corruption institutions (MEFP ¶38.c, e and g).
     Recently revised Civil Servant (Conduct) rules will support the online publication of asset
     declarations of high-level federal civil servants (end-December 2026 SB). Further revisions will
     specify limited disclosure restrictions on confidential personal information, and along with a
     framework for risk-based and timely verification, will facilitate the publication. To enhance
     independence and transparency of the National Accountability Bureau (NAB), the authorities will
     submit amendments on the NAB Ordinance to parliament to enhance the appointment process
     for the NAB chairman and publish investigation and prosecution rules and annual enforcement
     statistics (new proposed end-January 2027 SB). Finally, the Anti-Corruption and AML/CFT
     Committee will develop a methodology for corruption risk assessment to guide NAB’s
     development of an action plan to mitigate corruption risks in government agencies (proposed
     revised end-October 2026 SB). Methodological alignment as a preliminary step to the
     corruption risk assessment is critical to ensure its comprehensiveness and effective use of all
     relevant information sources.

•    Other governance reforms (MEFP ¶38). Progress in other areas includes publishing the full
     UNCAC review report on UNODC’s website9 and extending banks’ access to asset declarations to
     cover all public officials, with access statistics to be published by June 2026.

25.     Advancing SOE reforms and the privatization agenda are central to scaling back the
state’s footprint while boosting the governance, transparency and efficiency of SOEs. This
requires sustained political commitment and effort to strengthen institutional capacity, preserve the
integrity of hard-gained reforms and avoid future dilution of the SOE framework.




9
 https://track.unodc.org/uploads/documents/UNCAC/CountryVisitFinalReports/2026_02_16_Pakistan_Cycle_II_Country
_Review_Report_EN.pdf.


20   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                              PAKISTAN


•   Bringing SOEs in line with the SOE Act (MEFP ¶36a). The authorities sent 6 amendments to
    SOE-dedicated laws to parliament in January 2026 for approval. They now need to progress with
    the remaining 3 SOEs with dedicated laws (end-August 2026 SB). The authorities have also
    finalized and will soon submit draft SWF Law amendments to parliament. These amendments
    clarify the legal mandate of the SWF as a holding company, strengthen SWF governance, ensure
    that SWF-owned SOEs remain under the same high-quality governance structures and
    accountability standards as all other SOEs, require transparent and competitive procedures for
    divestment and procurement, and introduce key fiscal safeguards (MEFP ¶36b). The adoption of
    these amendments (end-March 2026 SB) without further delay is essential to maintain reform
    momentum. The operationalization of the SWF will remain on hold until these legal changes are
    enacted.

•   Implementing the SOE governance framework. Further progress has been made in
    implementing the SOE framework at the commercial SOEs to which the legal framework already
    applies (MEFP ¶36.c). Most commercial SOEs have published business plans, statements of
    corporate intent, and IFRS-compliant financial statements, in line with recent manuals and
    guidelines. It is critical that the Central Monitoring Unit (CMU), which is now fully staffed,
    exercises close surveillance over the quality of this reporting. Progress is ongoing on preparing
    and incorporating the public service obligation (PSO) agreements for the seven largest SOEs in
    the FY27 budget (end-June 2026 SB). Further refinement of the PSO guidelines, in consultation
    with the ADB, will allow incorporating new PSO agreements in the FY28 budget. Finally, progress
    has been made in establishing majority-independent Boards at commercial SOEs; this process
    should be completed at the remaining SOEs in the near future.

•   Advancing the privatization agenda. Two privatizations have been agreed upon since October
    2025, including the Pakistan International Airlines. The authorities should build on this positive
    momentum by finalizing the delayed appointment of a new financial advisor for the Roosevelt
    Hotel and moving forward with DISCO private sector participation.

26.     The authorities have committed to accelerating reforms focusing on removing
distortions and easing regulatory burdens:

•   Improving the Business Environment. The next phase of duty reductions under the National
    Tariff Policy will be legislated under the FY27 budget, and efforts to remove and simplify
    Pakistan’s extensive non-tariff barriers also need to move forward (MEFP ¶39). As part of the
    broader regulatory streamlining effort, a new national regulatory registry will be established as a
    comprehensive source on business regulations, starting with the federal government and ICT
    governments (new proposed end-June 2027 SB), which will improve transparency and reduce
    regulatory uncertainty (MEFP ¶40b). The authorities are also in the process of finalizing
    amendments to the Companies Act for submission to Parliament (end-June 2026 SB), focusing
    on reducing regulatory burdens and transaction costs and improving corporate governance and
    transparency for both listed and unlisted companies (MEFP ¶40c). Alongside these efforts to
    improve the business environment, the phase out of fiscal incentives for special economic zones
    (SEZs), special technology zones (STZs), and export processing zones (EPZs) by 2035 will reduce



                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND      21
PAKISTAN


       fiscal costs and level the playing field for trade and investment. These changes will be codified by
       enacting amendments to the SEZ and STZ Acts (new proposed end-June 2027 SB), and by
       eliminating the option for exporters in EPZs to sell a share of their goods domestically (MEFP
       ¶40a).

•      Liberalizing Commodity Markets. Reducing government intervention in the wheat and sugar
       markets is essential to remove distortions and support private investment, productivity, and
       efficiency. These changes will also strengthen price discovery, which is particularly important
       amid elevated international food price volatility. Governance arrangements for strategic wheat
       reserves should remain robust, with procurement conducted through the private sector at prices
       aligned with international markets and releases limited to cases of declaration of emergency. In
       the sugar sector, the authorities are making progress on adopting a new national policy agreed
       by federal and provincial governments (end‑June 2026 SB). The policy envisages removing
       zoning and licensing restrictions, ending administered cane and sugar pricing, liberalizing
       imports and exports under transparent rules within a sequenced implementation plan.

27.      Improvements to the macroeconomic statistics are also ongoing but should be
accompanied by enhanced transparency and PBS resources (MEFP ¶41). The PBS completed the
timely publication of full results from the FY25 Labor Force Survey (LFS) and Household Income and
Expenditure Survey (HIES) in early January 2026. Work is progressing on the producer price index
and on three major surveys originally planned for FY26, though timelines for these exercises were
delayed when the PBS was also tasked on an ad hoc basis with conducting a new national SME
survey. The Government Finance Statistics (GFS) compiled by the Finance Division remain subject to
shortcomings that undermine transparency, including a persistent statistical discrepancy between
above and below-the-line measures of the fiscal deficit. The authorities should accelerate work to
strengthen the GFS, by reporting spending on a GFSM 2014 basis, and broadening the coverage of
extrabudgetary units in the above-the-line statistics, which should help minimize the statistical
discrepancy. Following the identification of discrepancies in the trade data, procedures for import
data collection and aggregation are being strengthened, and the PBS will ensure full transparency on
the impact of these issues by publishing a full set of revised monthly and annual import statistics and
explanations by end-August 2026.



RSF POLICIES
28.        Implementation of climate resilience-enhancing reforms under the RSF is advancing:

•      RM7 and RM8 were implemented in December 2025. The SBP issued guidelines for the
       management of climate related financial risks (RM7).10 Based on the green taxonomy adopted in
       conjunction with the World Bank in August 2025, the SECP issued guidelines to enable listed
       companies to disclose climate-related risk and opportunities (RM8). Taken together, these
       measures represent a significant improvement in Pakistan’s climate information architecture,

10
     https://www.sbp.org.pk/smefd/circulars/2025/C7-Annexure.pdf


22      INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                              PAKISTAN


      which will help to facilitate climate resilience-focused investment. The implementation of these
      reforms will help to reduce prospective balance of payments risks by enhancing financial sector
      resilience via lower climate shock-related financial sector losses and enhanced climate adaptation
      infrastructure and external financing through climate investment facilitation.

•     Progress is being made on upcoming RMs. A recent IMF CD mission supported the authorities’
      development of a disaster risk financing framework that will establish a coordination mechanism
      across federal and provincial entities (RM6, end-August 2026). The authorities are in the
      process of revising their Public Investment Procedures and Parameters methodology to increase
      its climate change weighting (RM1, end-August 2026). Some progress has been made on work
      to replace the existing electricity subsidy structure with a targeted framework for low-income
      consumers (RM12, end-January 2027); it is critical that the authorities implement an effective
      nationwide communications campaign to prepare electricity consumers for the transition.


PROGRAM MODALITIES
29.      Updates to EFF-related conditionality are proposed to guide program implementation:

•     New quantitative targets. New QPCs are proposed for end-December 2026, and new ITs are
      proposed for end-September 2026, end-March-2027, and end-June 2027. The new targets are
      set to reflect the more limited scope for reserve accumulation in the current adverse
      environment, yet consistent with EFF objectives of supporting continued reserve accumulation
      over the program horizon, containing inflationary pressures, continuing fiscal adjustment and
      enhancing revenue mobilization. Given the criticality of domestic revenue mobilization to achieve
      the program objectives, and persistent slippages against the IT, the floor on net tax revenue
      collected by the FBR is proposed to be upgraded to a QPC from end-December 2026.

•     Structural Benchmarks. New SBs are proposed to monitor the program’s structural agenda,
      including on approval of the FY27 budget, improving revenue administration, strengthening the
      anti-corruption and public procurement frameworks, continuing regular adjustments to gas and
      power tariffs, maintaining the generosity of the UCT program, preparing a roadmap for gradual
      FX liberalization, and enhancing regulatory transparency and predictability.

30.      The program is fully financed, with firm commitments for the next 12 months and
good prospects for the remainder of the Fund-supported program (Text Table 3). Programmed
disbursements have largely developed as anticipated, with a planned US$250 million Panda bond
issuance expected to go ahead in FY26Q4. The repayment of deposits of US$3.5 billion to an official
bilateral creditor was fully financed by the provision of US$3 billion in SBP deposits by another major
official bilateral creditor and a 3-year $750 million Eurobond issued on April 16. The authorities also
reached an agreement for the extension of the maturity of US$5 billion in official bilateral deposits
from 1 to 3 years. Other official bilateral deposits have continued to be rolled over as anticipated.
Furthermore, commercial financing facilities could potentially be tapped if needed to cover any
additional external financing needs.

31.       Enterprise risks have increased but remain manageable. Financial risks have increased
somewhat, given the impact of the Middle East war on Pakistan’s economy and BOP but are
mitigated by the overperformance on reserve accumulation to date, and by phased access, burden
sharing, and adequate financing assurances. Strong program ownership going forward remains
critical to mitigate business and financial risks. The intensification of Pakistan’s conflict with
Afghanistan also poses increased operational risks, including to staff safety and wellbeing, which
continue to be mitigated through close coordination of Fund activities in line with policies and
supported by UNDSS. Reputational risks could arise from the interaction of geopolitical conflicts and
related supply side shocks with the Fund program and will be mitigated by clear communication and
continued close member engagement. Strong program ownership going forward remains critical to
mitigate business and financial risks.

32.     Safeguards. The 2023 Safeguards Assessment found a broadly sound safeguards framework,
with steady progress in implementation since. Remaining priorities include filling one senior
management position and amending the SBP Act at an opportune time to clarify the prohibition on
quasi-fiscal and developmental activities

33.     Pakistan's capacity to repay the Fund remains adequate but is critically dependent on
steadfast policy implementation and timely external financing. The Fund’s exposure would peak
at SDR 9,775 million in September 2027 (481 percent of quota and about 64 percent of projected
gross reserves in 2027). Risks to consistent policy implementation include resistance to adoption of
reforms, underperformance of tax revenue, high gross financing needs, low gross reserves, and the


24     INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                             PAKISTAN


sizeable net FX derivative position of the SBP, coupled with sociopolitical tensions, which could erode
repayment capacity and debt sustainability. Uncertainty about the duration of the war in the Middle
East and global geoeconomic and financial conditions in major trading partners adds to these risks.
Adequate and timely execution of the firm and credible financing assurances from official creditors
remains essential to mitigate these risks.


STAFF APPRAISAL
34.      Strong program implementation has bolstered Pakistan’s resilience to external shocks,
but reform momentum needs to be sustained against a challenging backdrop. Growth had
gained momentum prior to the Middle East war, as program implementation had begun to bear
results. However, the war weighs on the near-term outlook as Pakistan is highly exposed to energy
imports and remittances from the Gulf countries as well as to global financial conditions. An
extended period of volatile commodity prices, elevated global policy uncertainty, and higher
long-term interest rates could substantially impact its economy and the balance of payments. Amidst
this challenging international environment, and with growing domestic concerns about poverty and
growth, strong policy and reform efforts need to be sustained and deepened to lock in recent gains,
strengthen public finances, continue rebuilding external buffers, and support inclusive and resilient
private sector-led growth.

35.      The authorities’ commitment to the program fiscal targets amidst a difficult global
environment is commendable. The gradual fiscal consolidation envisaged in the program remains
appropriate to strengthen resilience in a shock prone world and reduce vulnerabilities from the still
high debt and gross financing needs. However, the consolidation progress so far has relied primarily
on increasing revenue from the formal sector. The authorities should scale up efforts to broaden the
tax base in the undertaxed sectors by strengthening revenue administration, which would also create
space to address distortions in the existing tax policy design and support long-term growth.
Spending efficiency and public financial management reforms are also key to support policy
credibility and sustainability. Enhancing social protection and human capital development are critical
to build resilience against shocks and improve poverty and employment indicators. In this regard, the
planned FY27 increase in UCT benefit generosity and in health and education spending are welcome,
and further efforts should be made to ensure their expansion and timely and quality implementation.

36.      The SBP should remain vigilant to ensure inflation expectations stay anchored in the
face of ongoing commodity price volatility, while allowing the exchange rate to act as a shock
absorber. With inflationary pressures likely to increase, the SBP should stand ready to tighten
monetary policy if needed, while carefully monitoring second-round effects on prices, wages and
inflation expectations. Efforts to continue improving central bank communication will help anchor
expectations and support effective policy implementation. Exchange rate flexibility should be the
main shock absorber, to preserve and continue building reserve buffers, which are still well below
comfortable levels. At the same time, the SBP should continue deepening the interbank FX market,
including through a carefully sequenced medium-term FX liberalization.




                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND      25
PAKISTAN


37.      The authorities should continue to proactively address financial sector vulnerabilities.
Recent progress in ensuring that all banks in the system are adequately capitalized are welcome.
Decisive actions are now needed to address microfinance banks’ capital shortfalls. Efforts should also
continue to advance capital market development, diversify the domestic debt investor base, improve
the private sector’s access to finance, and strengthen AML/CFT effectiveness. The regulation of
virtual assets should safeguard financial stability and ensure investor protection while harnessing
benefits. The post-2027 financial sector strategy should remove ambiguities about the
implementation of the recent constitutional amendment.

38.      Advancing and deepening structural reforms is the only way to sustainably boost long-
term growth. Delivering measurable outcomes of the EGR reform actions and strengthening the
effectiveness, transparency, and independence of anti-corruption institutions would help reduce risks
of undue influence in policymaking and implementation and foster a more stable and investor-
friendly business environment. Continuing to move forward on the power and gas structural reform
agendas, while keeping tariff structures in line with costs and progressive to protect vulnerable
consumers, remains the only path to energy sector viability. Advancing SOE reforms and the
privatization agenda is central to scaling back the state’s footprint and improving service delivery
and requires sustained political commitment and efforts to strengthen institutional capacity. Efforts
to enable private sector development should accelerate and remain focused on eliminating
distortions and easing unnecessary regulations. Efforts to improve macroeconomic statistics should
be accompanied by improvements in transparency while safeguarding PBS resources.

39.     Progress made so far on the climate agenda needs to be maintained. RMs 7 and 8 have
been completed, with a combined associated disbursement amount of SDR 153.8 million. The
momentum from the completion of the first four RMs during this and the first review must be
maintained to boost resilience to Pakistan’s large climate-related macroeconomic vulnerabilities. This
will require advance planning for RMs targeted for 2027, close coordination with provinces where
applicable, and engagement with Fund and other development partners for technical advice where
necessary.

40.   Based on the authorities’ program implementation and policy commitments, staff
supports their request for completion of the third review under the EFF arrangement and for
completion of the second review under the RSF arrangement.

 1/ As reported by country authorities according to their classification of creditors, including by official and commercial. Debt coverage includes guaranteed debt and debt for commodity operations
 by provincial governments. Debt service is reported based on the outstanding stock at end-June 2025.
 2/ Nominal GDP reported and used for shares of debt-to-GDP is for FY25 (FY: July 1 to June 30).
 3/ Multilateral creditors” are simply institutions with more than one official shareholder and may not necessarily align with creditor classification under other IMF policies (e.g. Lending Into Arrears).

 4/ Debt is collateralized when the creditor has rights over an asset or revenue stream that would allow it, if the borrower defaults on its payment obligations, to rely on the asset or revenue stream
 to secure repayment of the debt. Collateralization entails a borrower granting liens over specific existing assets or future receivables to a lender as security against repayment of the loan. Collateral
 is “unrelated” when it has no relationship to a project financed by the loan. An example would be borrowing to finance the budget deficit, collateralized by oil revenue receipts. See the joint IMF-
 World Bank note for the G20 “Collateralized Transactions: Key Considerations for Public Lenders and Borrowers” for a discussion of issues raised by collateral.
 5/ Work is ongoing to improve the risk monitoring framework of Public Private Partnerships (PPP), including at the provincial level, supported by development partners.

    Annex I. Sovereign Risk and Debt Sustainability Analysis

                                   Table 1. Pakistan: Risk of Sovereign Stress

Debt stabilization in the baseline                                                        Yes
                                              DSA Summary Assessment
Staff commentary: Public debt continues to be assessed as sustainable in the baseline scenario underpinned by
steadfast implementation of the agreed EFF policies, with fiscal consolidation continuing in FY26 and FY27, and growth
inching further towards potential. Recent improvements with lowering GFNs need to continue, including by deepening
domestic debt markets, and timely disbursements of committed bilateral and multilateral support remain critical to
strengthen reserve buffers. Key risks include fading reform momentum and possible policy reversals, pressures on the
exchange rate, and a flattening of the growth trajectory (due to insufficient structural reforms or overly tight macro
policies). Contingent liabilities related to SOEs, especially in the energy sector, pose significant risks to debt
sustainability.


Source: Fund staff.
Note: The risk of sovereign stress is a broader concept than debt sustainability. Unsustainable debt can only be resolved
through exceptional measures (such as debt restructuring). In contrast, a sovereign can face stress without its debt
necessarily being unsustainable, and there can be various measures—that do not involve a debt restructuring—to remedy
such a situation, such as fiscal adjustment and new financing.
1/ The near-term assessment is not applicable in cases where there is a disbursing IMF arrangement. In surveillance-only
cases or in cases with precautionary IMF arrangements, the near-term assessment is performed but not published.
2/ A debt sustainability assessment is optional for surveillance-only cases and mandatory in cases where there is a Fund
arrangement. The mechanical signal of the debt sustainability assessment is deleted before publication. In surveillance-
only cases or cases with IMF arrangements with normal access, the qualifier indicating probability of sustainable debt
("with high probability" or "but not with high probability") is deleted before publication.
