 consist of: (i) guarantees, including for SOE's commodity operations and PHPL (PRs 3,963 billion), (ii) non-guaranteed circular debt in the power
 and gas sector (PRs 931 billion, excluding PHPL debt, and PRs 3,183 billion), (iii) non-guaranteed external SOE debt (PRs 144 billion), (iv) other
 contingent liabilities (PRs 500 billion, estimated). The estimated total of those exposures amounts to 7.7 percent of GDP.

Staff commentary: The fiscal adjustment path over the remainder of the program is feasible given the significant scope for
revenues by widening the tax base and improving tax administration. But strong efforts on structural reforms remain essential
to lift long-term growth to underpin revenue collection. T-bill issuance as a share of total domestic issuance is projected to
decline gradually as the domestic investor base widens.

projected debt-to-GDP ratio.

 Staff commentary: Of the two medium-term tools, the Debt Fanchart Module points to a moderate level of risk, with relatively high debt-to-GDP
 at the end of the projection horizon and some risk of debt non-stabilization. The GFN Financeability Module suggests a high level of risk due to
 the high share of bank assets invested in government securities (which limits the scope for the government to flexibly respond to future crises)
 and the average GFN-to-GDP ratio above the 75th percentile of the comparator group. The aggregated index continues to point to a high level
 of risk.

 5/ The signal is low risk if the GFI is below 0.26; high risk if the DFI is above 0.40; and otherwise, it is moderate risk.

Commentary: Although elevated GFN are a key risk for Pakistan's debt sustainability, the risk of large amortizations is
not triggered because projected GFN-to-GDP and Amortization-to-GDP ratios for years t+6 through t+30 do not exceed
the historical average (plus one standard deviation) threshold given elevated historical time series over the past 10 years.
The baseline scenario assumes a zero primary balance from 2031 onwards, which would allow Pakistan to expand
development spending to address unmet development needs. Under this projection, which is predicated on
macroeconomic stability remaining firmly entrenched, debt (in percent of GDP) would continue to decline.

                           scenario)                                                                                         scenario)
                           With climate adaptation (customized                                                               With climate adaptation (customized
                           scenario)                                                                                         scenario)
           Commentary: Given Pakistan's exceptionally high vulnerability to climate change, freeing up fiscal space for
           adaptation investment within the confines of debt sustainability will be critical. The customized scenario assumes
           adaption investment of 1 percent of GDP annually over a five-year period (see CR 25/109) whereas the standardized
           scenario reflects adaptation costs of only 0.6 percent of GDP annually. By reducing the growth impact of natural
           disaster significantly, higher adaptation investment would help support the relatively modest increase in the debt-to-
           GDP ratio relative to baseline.

           Commentary: Estimates of mitigation costs by development partners amount to 5.6 percent of GDP annually over
           the 2023-30 period, very close to the 5.7 percent of GDP under the standardized scenario. Given fiscal and
           implementation constraints the customized scenario distributes these costs over a 20-year period. The massive
           increase in the debt-to-GDP ratio relative to baseline, to more than 80 percent of GDP by the mid-2050s, points to the
           need for a well thought-out climate financing strategy and support from bilateral and multilateral partners.

1
  The Risk Assessment Matrix (RAM) shows events that could materially alter the baseline path (the scenario most
likely to materialize in the view of IMF staff). The relative likelihood is the staff’s subjective assessment of the risks
surrounding the baseline (“low” is meant to indicate a probability below 10 percent, “medium” a probability between
10–30 percent, and “high” a probability between 30–50 percent). The RAM reflects staff views on the source of risks
and overall level of concern as of the time of discussions with the authorities. Non-mutually exclusive risks may
interact and materialize jointly. “Short term” and “medium term” are meant to indicate that the risk could materialize
within one year and three years, respectively.

                    Annex III. Illustration of the RSF Ambition Summary Table

                               Table 1. Pakistan: Illustration of the RSF Ambition-Summary Table
Staff assessed that making progress towards reducing Pakistan’s prospective balance of payments risks from climate change requires
adopting fiscal, financial, and institutional policy reforms, including in specific macro-critical economic sectors. Reform areas covered by the
RSF include: (i) mainstreaming climate considerations in policymaking, including PFM/PIM; (ii) enhancing water system resilience and disaster
response financing; (iii) strengthening the climate information architecture and management climate-related financial risks; (iv) promoting
green mobility and transport decarbonization; and (v) aligning energy sector reforms with national mitigation commitments.

The table below shows an assessment of the current status each of these reform areas. It includes an assessment of current policies or
institutional frameworks (left column) and the desired long-term policy outcome (third column). RSF-supported reform areas (both RMs and
commitments in the MEFP), represented by the green arrow (second column), are targeted at helping the country achieve these outcomes. To
fully achieve the recommended policy outcomes, additional reforms will be necessary. Some of the possible additional reforms, drawn from
available diagnostics, are reported in the blue arrow. These reflect a mix of reforms that may be supported by development partners or
carried forward independently by the authorities—in synergy with and building off the momentum of the RSF-supported reforms; however,
they do not necessarily represent authorities’ commitments. Strong synergies with the current EFF arrangement, such as domestic revenue
mobilization, public financial management, energy sector reforms, and financial market deepening, should further enhance the impact of
expected outcomes under RSF-supported reforms.

The relative length of the green arrow compared to the “full set” of reforms provides a proxy of the progress expected to be possible directly
through RSF-supported reforms. This reflects the authorities’ ambition (cognizant of their capacity) under the RSF arrangement as well as
limits to the Fund’s expertise in certain areas. For example, the Fund’s expertise in areas such as agriculture and land management is limited
while development partners are better placed to provide this support.

                              Appendix I. Letter of Intent

Ms. Kristalina Georgieva                                              Islamabad, April 23, 2026
Managing Director
International Monetary Fund
700 19th Street, N.W.
Washington, D.C. 20431
USA

Dear Ms. Georgieva:

1.       Our economic program, supported by the Extended Arrangement under the Extended Fund
Facility (EFF) and the Resilience and Sustainability Facility (RSF), continues to deliver macroeconomic
stability, build resilience, and set the stage for sustainable and inclusive growth. After weathering
significant floods in 2025, the economy continues to grow, inflation remains stable, and external and
fiscal buffers are being rebuilt. However, the conflict in the Middle East has resulted in higher
international commodity prices and tighter global financial conditions, making the outlook for the
coming period more challenging.

2.      Our performance under the EFF remains strong. We met all end-December 2025
Quantitative Performance Criteria (QPCs), strongly overperforming the floor on net international
reserves and comfortably meeting the general government’s primary balance target. We also met six
of eight end-December 2025 Indicative Targets (ITs). Net tax revenues collected by the FBR and
income tax revenues from retailers fell short of our indicative targets, and we remain focused on
implementing revenue administration reforms to minimize the shortfall by the end of the fiscal year.

3.      Good progress has also been made on our structural reform agenda. We met five structural
benchmarks (SBs) in the areas of tax administration, governance, social support, gas sector
sustainability, and special technology zones on time. Due to the need for extensive technical
consultations, we missed the deadline for the SB on Sovereign Wealth Fund (SWF) governance, but
made good progress towards meeting the substance of this commitment as the draft law will soon
be submitted to Parliament. Further progress was made on Reform Measures (RMs) under the RSF:
We adopted a green taxonomy and issued guidelines on the management of climate-related
financial risks (RM7) and on listed companies’ disclosure of climate related risks and opportunities
(RM8). The tax exemption for sugar imports was extended, and thus the continuous SB on not
granting preferential tax treatment was missed. The exemption was subsequently repealed, with no
imports utilizing it. We remain committed to commodity market reforms.

4.      We remain firmly committed to continuing with sound and prudent macroeconomic policies
and structural and institutional reforms to place Pakistan on a path toward long-term sustainable
and inclusive growth, providing the crucial foundation to withstand shocks and address their fallout
comprehensively and effectively. In the months ahead, we will continue with (i) the prudent
execution of the FY26 budget, including by implementing necessary revenue measures to minimize
the revenue shortfalls; (iii) formulation of the FY27 budget, in close consultation with Fund staff, to
serve as our fiscal anchor with an underlying primary surplus of 2.0 percent of GDP; (iii) appropriate


60   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                              PAKISTAN


monetary policy to ensure that inflation stays durably within the target range and a flexible
exchange rate to buffer shocks; (iv) regular energy tariff adjustments that maintain a progressive
tariff structure and shield the most vulnerable from large tariff increases, and cost-reducing reforms
in the energy sector; and (v) reforms to reduce the footprint of the state, remove market distortions
and excessive regulatory burdens, and strengthen governance.

5.       Reflecting our progress in advancing the program goals against the backdrop of an
exceptionally challenging environment, we request approval by the IMF Executive Board for (i) new
QPCs for end-December 2026, including on net tax revenues collected by the FBR; (ii) the
completion of the third review under the Extended Arrangement and the related purchase in the
amount of SDR 760 million; and (iii) the completion of the second review under the Resilience and
Sustainability Facility and the disbursement linked to implementation of RM7 and RM8 (SDR 153.8
million in total), to be provided as budget support to create fiscal space to address our economic
vulnerabilities and increase economic resilience, including by adapting our economy to withstand
the impact of climate change. We have also made good progress in realizing the financing
commitments of our main official and bilateral partners.

6.       The policies set forth in the attached Memorandum of Economic and Financial Policies will
guide the successful implementation of our program in the face of multiple adverse shocks and
ongoing exceptional uncertainty. All provinces agree that they will not introduce any policy or action
which could be considered to undermine or run against any of the commitments or policies outlined
in this letter or the attached MEFP. We will monitor closely economic developments and
performance and will stand ready to take additional measures that may become necessary to
achieve our program objectives. We will consult with the IMF before modifying the measures
contained in the MEFP or before adopting new measures that deviate from the goals of the
program, in accordance with the Fund’s policies on such consultations. All provinces agree to
consult with the IMF through the federal Ministry of Finance before modifying or adopting any
measures that could affect or undercut the program specified here or which deviates from the goals
of the program. We will supply the IMF with timely and accurate data and draft policy documents
needed for program monitoring. The understandings between us and the IMF regarding
performance criteria and structural measures described in this letter are further specified in the
attached Technical Memorandum of Understanding. We consent to the IMF’s publication of this
letter, the MEFP, the TMU, and the accompanying Executive Board documents.

Sincerely yours,

                        /s/                                              /s/
             Muhammad Aurangzeb                                     Jameel Ahmad
         Minister of Finance and Revenue                 Governor of the State Bank of Pakistan


 Attachments:      Memorandum of Economic and Financial Policies, Technical Memorandum of
                   Understanding




                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND      61
PAKISTAN


    Attachment I. Memorandum of Economic and Financial Policies
This memorandum supplements our memorandum of November 20, 2025, provides information on
recent developments, documents our achievements, and discusses further policy steps and reforms
needed to meet our IMF-supported program objectives.

A. Recent Economic Developments and Outlook

1.       The economic recovery has gained momentum, though the conflict in the Middle East
has clouded the outlook. GDP grew by 3.7 percent (yoy) in FY26 Q1, driven by a strong expansion
in the industrial sector. Inflation has increased in recent months, but has stayed in the SBP’s target
range, while core inflation has remained contained. The current account recorded a deficit of
US$0.7 billion (0.2 percent of annual GDP) in July-February FY26, as imports continued to rebound
while food exports fell back due to increased rice competition in international markets. Thanks to
continued inflows and foreign exchange (FX) purchases by the State Bank of Pakistan (SBP), gross
reserves increased to US$16.3 billion at end-February and have remained broadly stable thereafter.

2.      However, the conflict in the Middle East has pushed commodity prices up sharply,
which will put upward pressure on inflation and weigh on growth and the current account.
Reflecting recent developments, the agreed policy stance, and ongoing reforms, we see the
following baseline macroeconomic scenario:

•    Real GDP growth is now expected in the range of 3 ½ - 4 percent in FY26, reflecting continued
     momentum in the auto, construction, and garment industries. Growth is projected to remain at a
     similar level in FY27, as higher fuel prices and weaker external demand weigh on the ongoing
     recovery. Assuming the conflict is resolved quickly, the implementation of economic policies
     geared to entrenching macroeconomic stability, boosting external competitiveness, and
     improving the private sector business environment are expected to support a gradual pickup in
     medium-term growth to 4½ percent, bolstered by increased investment and exports.

•    Headline CPI inflation is expected to temporarily exceed the target toward the end of FY26 due
     to adverse energy and food prices, but appropriately tight monetary policy is expected to
     support a durable return of headline CPI inflation to the target range in FY28.

•    The current account is projected to return to a modest deficit, reflecting higher global energy
     prices due to the conflict in the Middle East and elevated import growth following the impact
     from the 2025 floods. Over the medium-term, the current account is expected to remain at a
     modest deficit following the expected normalization of import demand, the impact of the National
     Tariff Policy, and a moderation of remittance inflows.

3.       Risks to the baseline remain high. Recurring natural disasters pose significant risks to
domestic and external stability. The global environment remains highly uncertain. An escalation of
regional tensions and a prolonged conflict in the Middle East could adversely impact external
stability via higher commodity prices, lower growth, and diminished risk appetite of foreign


62   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                PAKISTAN


investors. In the event that the supply of fuel imports to Pakistan is more severely disrupted,
downside impacts on growth could be larger. At the same time, enhanced cooperation with key
bilateral partners provides upside risk to foreign investment flows and external support.

B. Economic Program

Fiscal Policy

4.      We remain committed to our FY26 underlying primary balance target of 1.6 percent of
GDP. The general government primary surplus reached PRs 4.1 trillion in the first half of FY26
(3.2 percent of GDP, comfortably meeting the end-December 2025 QPC of 3.3 trillion). However, the
Federal Board of Revenue (FBR) missed its end-December indicative revenue target, with net tax
revenues reaching PRs 6.2 trillion against targeted PRs 6.5 trillion (a shortfall of 0.2 percent of GDP).
We attribute the shortfall largely to declining collections in the power, as well as oil and gas, sectors.
Around half of the shortfall in FBR revenues was offset by higher PDL and provincial revenues, lower
than expected costs of flood response, and loan repayments from SOEs stemming from the power CD
settlement scheme. We recognize that urgent actions are required to mitigate the impact of projected
tax revenue shortfall and ensure the achievement of the FY26 underlying primary balance target of
1.6 percent of GDP.

5.      To this end, as a prior action we will:

•    Collect additional tax revenues stemming from recent court rulings. We will collect
     PRs 322 billion (0.3 percent of GDP) from disputed taxpayer obligations on which the courts
     have ruled in favor of the FBR. Most of this amount is from the recent court ruling confirming
     the legality of the “super tax”. Prompt enforcement of court rulings will bolster government
     revenues this fiscal year and signal our strong commitment to revenue mobilization.

•    Ensure that expected savings in current expenditure of the federal government materialize
     by issuing a decision by the Finance Secretary on FY26 savings affecting revised estimates of
     expenditure ceilings, which will be placed before the National Assembly along with the FY27
     budget. We envisage that PRs 136 billion (0.1 percent of GDP) will be saved largely from lower-
     than-expected grants to provinces to cover the costs of flood response.

6.       The exceptional volatility in oil prices required urgent measures to protect affected
households. To ensure adequate fiscal space for such support, we have introduced a package of
saving measures yielding PRs 152 billion comprising: (i) a reduction of fuel allowances for official
vehicles and a 20 percent cut in FY26Q4 non-salary expenditures (PRs 27 billion), (ii) PRs 100 billion
cut in the PSDP allocation and (iii) PRs 25 billion in grants from SOEs outside the general
government. These resources have allowed us to delay the revision of fuel prices. We recognize that
such support can only be temporary, and regular adjustment of fuel prices plays a crucial role in
helping demand respond to changing fuel market conditions and avoid very costly budgetary
subsidies. To this end, as a prior action we will allow the full alignment of domestic fuel prices with
international prices followed by regular fortnightly adjustments. To mitigate the impact on the most


                                                                    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       63
PAKISTAN


vulnerable, we are working with the provincial governments to provide financing for targeted and
sustainable support to affected households. Furthermore, considering the high volatility of oil and
fertilizer prices and the general macroeconomic uncertainty, we will postpone the increase in the
FED on fertilizers and pesticides. We are mindful of the strategic nature of fertilizers and the many
uncertainties faced by the agricultural sector.

7.      We remain committed to our fiscal consolidation efforts to ensure a sustainable
medium-term fiscal position and reduce public debt toward 70 percent of GDP. To this end, we
will develop an FY27 budget targeting an underlying primary balance of 2 percent of GDP (new
end-June 2026 SB). The budget will ensure:

•    FBR Revenue targets consistent with further revenue mobilization. We will adopt a
     combination of permanent tax policy and revenue administration measures yielding 0.3 percent
     of GDP in FY27. Drawing on previous IMF CD reports’ recommendations, we will reduce income
     and sales tax expenditures yielding 0.15 percent of GDP of revenues. Another 0.15 percent of
     GDP will be achieved by implementing our transformation plan (see ¶10). With these measures,
     we expect to achieve revenues of PRs 7,022 billion by end-December 2026, which will be set as a
     new quantitative performance criterion. The impact of any potential revenue-reducing tax
     simplification policies introduced in the FY27 budget ahead of the implementation of the
     medium-term tax reform strategy (MTRS) will be offset by new permanent tax policy measures
     with equivalent revenue yield.

•    Strict expenditure ceilings. We will restrict the growth of most primary current expenditures to
     no faster than the projected rate of inflation of 8.4 percent. Health, education, and social
     protection expenditures will continue to increase as a share of GDP in line with program targets
     to support human capital development. A specific lower ceiling will be set for power subsidies
     (see ¶32.b). These measures will save 0.1 percent of GDP in FY27.

•    Provincial surpluses. The budget will also include provincial tax revenue targets aiming to
     contribute an additional 0.3 percentage points of GDP to the tax revenue ratio and
     corresponding primary surplus targets sufficient to achieve the general government primary
     balance target. Provinces will mobilize revenue by continuing to steadfastly expand the
     enforcement of the GST on services to gradually cover all sectors of the economy. New
     agricultural income tax rates will be applied to FY26 agricultural income (with the revenue
     impact materializing in FY27).

8.       Steadfast implementation of fiscal institutional reforms remains critical to achieve our
fiscal consolidation objectives. Our primary focus continues to be on strengthening revenue
mobilization, enhancing the burden-sharing between the federal and provincial governments, and
further improving public financial management.




64   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                               PAKISTAN


Strengthening Revenue Mobilization

9.     The FBR has implemented priority areas of the transformation plan (end-March 2026
SB) and developed key performance indicators that will be monitored throughout the rest of
the program:

a. Strengthening taxpayer audits. The FBR has now begun applying the Compliance Risk
   Management (CRM) system for corporate and non-corporate taxpayers. As of March, 2026 the
   FBR has introduced a new audit workflow under which all audit cases will be high risk cases
   identified through CRM (training more than 150 officers in its use). Local tax commissioners will
   assign the cases to either existing field officers or newly hired auditors. The FBR has hired
   431 auditors by end-March 2026 and will hire an additional 396 by end-June 2026. Rejecting
   cases identified by CRM will only be possible in specific documented circumstances (e.g. a
   recently concluded audit, pending legal challenges, etc.). The timeliness and quality of audits will
   be centrally monitored with specific accountability for delaying the process or poor audit quality.
   The stronger audit process is expected to deliver PRs 92 billion in additional revenue in FY2027.
   The effectiveness of the new process will be monitored with two key performance indicators
   (KPI): (i) value of audit adjustments (excluding any penalties or interest) which are paid within
   (120) days of the end of the audit (broken down on an office basis and with separate
   calculations for LTO cases and other cases); and (ii) number of cases where there is an audit
   adjustment paid within (120) days, as a percentage of all cases completed (broken down on an
   office by office basis and with separate calculations for LTO and other cases).

b. Expanding the application of digital invoicing. By December 31st, 2025, all entities registered
   as sales tax filers were required to register in the FBR's digital invoicing platform. As of end-
   March around one third of the tax filers were issuing live invoices. To facilitate the adoption of
   the system more broadly, FBR will issue two notifications allowing for easier correction of errors
   and multiple license integrators by end-March 2026. We will also submit legal amendments in
   the upcoming Finance Bill to require more stringent penalties for non-compliance. Active sales
   taxpayers are expected to adopt the system by July 31st, 2026. Pakistan Revenue Automation
   Limited (PRAL) has assigned 16 people to help operate the new system. The new digital
   invoicing system simplifies sales tax filing for taxpayers and enables automatic calculation of
   sales tax liability. Better monitoring and calculation of sales tax liability is expected to generate
   PRs 46 billion in additional revenue in FY27. The operation of the new invoicing system will be
   monitored by using KPIs on: (i) the total value of digital invoices processed by the invoicing
   system and (ii) the number of sales taxpayers issuing live invoices in the system.

c. Production monitoring. FBR is deploying production monitoring in sectors with the largest tax
   gaps (notably textiles). A production monitoring solution has been developed for all targeted
   sectors (including piloting proofs of concept), with full deployment in the sugar, cement,
   tobacco and fertilizer sectors (PRs 160 billion estimated tax gap), and with the textile and
   beverage sectors in pilot phase and expected to be fully monitored by end-October 2026. FBR
   assigned 200 people to help operate the production monitoring systems. Monitoring production
   quantities is expected to increase reported revenues in sales tax returns and generate


                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       65
PAKISTAN


     PRs 48 billion in additional revenue in FY27. This intervention will be assessed by using KPIs on:
     (i) the increase in sales tax revenues from monitored locations relative to the revenue of the
     previous year adjusted for nominal GDP growth; and (ii) the volume of goods identified in
     monitored locations.

10.     The FBR continues to strengthen its internal governance. To further enhance the audit
process and ensure even-handed treatment of taxpayers across different regions and industries, in
line with international best practice, the FBR will centralize the audit case selection process via
administrative prioritization and monitoring of high-risk cases through the Compliance Risk
Management system (new end-August 2026 SB). As part of the structural benchmark, the FBR will
adopt a standardized audit manual and a published audit policy. The newly established Performance
Review and CRM (PRCRM) committee will submit to the Chief Commissioners and Member Audit
FBR Board a monthly early warning report describing how local offices comply with the audit policy
and any follow-up actions for non-compliance. The audit policy will require mandatory follow up of
all high-risk cases identified through CRM, which should include risks on registration and non-filing.
The audit policy will also describe how to deal with cases referred to the FBR by third parties. Lack of
CRM follow up will be permitted only under explicit and narrow criteria specified in the audit policy
and subject to documented justification and supervisory approval per the PCRM committee
mandate. The policy will require establishing and regularly monitoring outcome-oriented audit
performance indicators (see above).

11.      Our Tax Policy Office is actively working on the medium-term tax reform strategy
(end-December 2026 SB). TPO management is in place, and the office expects to reach full
capacity of 35-36 permanent staff by end-May 2026. Temporary staff are currently working to set up
modeling infrastructure. The office has conducted extensive outreach to the business community
across the country aiming to solicit input to the MTRS. The MTRS will be based on: (i) careful
calibration to ensure no loss of tax revenue (revenue neutrality); (ii) detailed planning of tax reforms
to ensure policy, social, and legal soundness; and (iii) clear communication to set adequate
expectations of the medium-term timing and long-term stability of the tax policy reforms. A data
sharing arrangement governing the relationship between the TPO and FBR has been signed in
March 2026.

Enhancing Fiscal Burden-Sharing with Provincial Governments

12.      The provinces have made important contributions to domestic revenue mobilization.
Provincial tax revenues have been increasing well above nominal GDP growth rates, reflecting
largely the tax base expansion of GST on services and stronger enforcement efforts. However,
revenues from agricultural income tax fell short of expectations because of delays in the application
of revised rates and other implementation challenges. The FBR has begun sharing income tax
information with the provinces to support the collection of provincial revenues in FY27.




66    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                               PAKISTAN


Enhancing Public Financial Management

13.     We have made the budget preparation process more transparent. We have started to
provide additional information in the quarterly fiscal operation reports, comparing actual outcomes
with projections. Additionally, the mid-year and year-end budget execution reports now include a
more detailed analysis of the key drivers behind spending deviations. The budget circular now
includes the latest macroeconomic projections.

14.       We have finalized a new project appraisal framework for the Public Sector
Development Program, which will be presented to ECNEC for approval by end-June 2026. We
expect that new project allocations will be less than 10 percent of the total in the FY27 budget, as
much of the limited fiscal space will be needed to complete existing projects. We are revising our
scorecard-based system for project selection to: (i) reduce the number of overlapping criteria;
(ii) introduce negative marking for projects with negative externalities; and (iii) increase the weight
on climate-related criteria (see ¶44.b).

15.     We will ensure transparency in all public procurement at the federal and provincial
levels through the electronic Pakistan Acquisition and Disposal System (e-PADS). The external
audit of the e-PADS has been finalized by the Auditor General of Pakistan. The Pakistan Public
Procurement Regulatory Authority (PPRA) is expanding the e-PADS to all federal agencies and three
provincial governments. It has rolled out an upgraded version of e-PADS, aiming to integrate all
agencies into the new system by end-December 2026. Alongside this, we will encourage major SOEs
to develop procurement rules with the objective of eventually bringing all SOE procurement to the
e-PADS. Furthermore, the PPRA has published beneficial ownership information of awarded
contracts larger than PRs 50 million. To level the playing field, we have prepared amendments of the
relevant provisions of the PPRA Rules to eliminate SOE preferences in awarding public procurement
contracts without competition, subject to limited and reasonable exceptions (new end-September
2026 SB).

16.     We have improved the risk monitoring framework of Public Private Partnerships
(PPP), including those at the provincial level. The Risk Management Unit (RMU) in the Ministry of
Finance, in coordination with other stakeholders and with support from the ADB, has finalized and
published the fiscal risk monitoring framework for quantifying contingent liabilities related to PPPs.
As of end-December 2025, we estimate the outstanding contingent liabilities at the federal and
provincial government levels to be PRs 472 billion. We will update and disclose this information at
least annually.

17.      We continue our work on consolidating cash holdings of government institutions.
To ensure consistency with Pakistan’s legal framework, we will not conduct a sectorization study but,
as a first step and in consultation with IMF staff, the TSA will include public entities defined in the
PFM Act of 2019. Around 70 new accounts with average balances of PRs 150 billion will be covered
under the TSA, which already includes 179 accounts with about PRs 140 billion in balances.




                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       67
PAKISTAN


Debt Management

18.      We are committed to addressing debt vulnerabilities stemming from elevated gross
financing needs and a significant sovereign-bank nexus. We continue to implement our debt
management strategy for FY26–28, aiming to rebalance the domestic debt portfolio toward longer-
term, fixed-rate instruments. Transparency has been enhanced regarding the provincial participation
in government securities auctions. We will continue to implement our plan to gradually retire
domestic debt held by the SBP. We are also making progress in strengthening the institutional
arrangements for public debt management.

19.      We continue prioritizing the development of the domestic government securities
market and widening the investor base. We have intensified our efforts to enhance investor
relations by actively informing current and potential investors about our debt management strategy.
We are evaluating new potential instruments that cater to investor preferences while carefully
considering associated costs and risks. We will refrain from any issuance that could expose the
government’s debt portfolio to excessive risks. We are about to finalize plans to reform our retail
debt program and are actively exploring digital channels to distribute debt securities more
efficiently. Furthermore, we remain committed to conducting a comprehensive study, in line with
guidance from international institutions, to assess the key pillars of the government securities
market and to publish a strategic action plan aimed at addressing identified bottlenecks, (end-
September 2026 SB).

Poverty Reduction and Social Protection

20.      We continue our efforts to tackle the challenges in the social sector. While our efforts
have helped mitigate the impact of the recent floods and other external shocks on the most
vulnerable, poverty remains high, with 40 percent of the population facing vulnerability, as does
unemployment, especially among young people. Rising emigration, including of skilled labor,
reflects limited domestic opportunities. Large human capital constraints – particularly regarding
education, health, and WASH outcomes – continue to weigh on productivity.

21.     To this end, and to protect the most vulnerable from the recent volatility in food and
fuel prices, we commit to significantly improve the generosity, coverage, and capacity of
Benazir Income Support (BISP) programs and provincial and federal health and education
expenditure amid necessary fiscal consolidation.

a.   Unconditional cash transfers (UCT). We have successfully implemented an annual inflation
     adjustment for the quarterly unconditional cash transfer (UCT) Kafaalat benefit and will have
     added an additional 200,000 families to the program by end-FY26, bringing total enrollment
     to 10.2 million families. Guided by the recently updated Household Integrated Economic
     Survey (HIES), our FY27 budget will allow space for an increase in the Kafaalat benefit from
     PRs 14,500 to PRs 18,000 beginning in January 2027 (new end-January 2027 SB). This will
     cover both projected inflation for 2026 and an additional increase in generosity, bringing
     quarterly benefits significantly closer to our goal of 15 percent of the lowest family income


68   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                            PAKISTAN


     quintile’s consumption basket. We are committed to continued inflation adjustment of the
     Kafaalat benefit beyond FY27, to maintain at a minimum its value in real terms for recipient
     families.

b.   Conditional cash transfers (CCT). We significantly increased enrollment in our two main CCT
     programs over FY26, by 700,000 to 11.4 million for the Taleemi health and education program
     and by 200,000 to 2.2 million for the Nashonuma nutrition program. Our FY27 budget will
     allow space to maintain spending as a share of GDP and enrollment for these two programs,
     and for the addition of a new CCT program targeting skills development. We will continue
     working closely with the World Bank and provinces to avoid overlap between BISP and
     provincial CCT programs at the district level.

c.   BISP administration. We have made further progress in improving our payments mechanism.
     We have created Social Protection Wallets (SPWs) for 7 million Kafaalat beneficiary families
     and distributed SIM cards to those beneficiaries with mobile phones and will progressively
     increase coverage of SPWs among secure wallets for all remaining families by end-July 2026.
     This will allow for the disbursement of benefits through digital accounts starting in Q4 FY26.
     We are also working with the SBP to implement agent level interoperability among banks used
     by beneficiaries. These measures will improve the transparency, disbursement efficiency, and
     convenience of benefits.

22.      We will continue to scale up critical health and education spending at the federal and
provincial levels. We are committed to fully executing all provincial and federal health and
education budgets for FY26, which would deliver a 0.3 percent of GDP increase in spending this
fiscal year. We will target another increase in aggregate spending of 0.2 percent of GDP to
PRs 4,227 billion in FY27 budgets. We will continue to work with development partners to identify
spending bottlenecks and improve execution efficiency to better meet quarterly intra-year spending
targets and the quality of health and education services provision.

Monetary, Exchange Rate, and Financial Sector Policies

23.      In the context of escalating regional conflicts, we are maintaining an appropriately
tight monetary policy stance and stand ready to raise interest rates if needed. As flood-related
risks dissipated, we were able to lower the policy rate by 50bps in December 2025, but with the
onset of the conflict in the Middle East, we held the policy rate in our March 9 meeting. We are
closely monitoring the recent volatility in global food and fuel prices and the pass-through to
domestic prices and core inflation, and stand ready to adjust policy, as needed. At the same time, we
are continuing efforts to improve our policy framework and strengthen communication, including by
providing greater detail on the MPC’s monetary policy considerations in the monetary policy report.
By end-June 2026, we will further assess how MPC statements and minutes could more clearly
(i) convey the committee’s assessment of the current and desired policy stance and (ii) characterize
the monetary policy reaction function in light of the committee’s risk assessment.




                                                                 INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND        69
PAKISTAN


24.     A flexible exchange rate, underpinned by an active foreign exchange market, is critical
to buffer shocks and rebuild reserves. We continue to view exchange rate flexibility as the key
shock absorber, including against spillovers from the Middle East conflict, which will ensure that BOP
pressures do not impact banks’ ability to accommodate import financing and other outward
payments on a timely basis. To this end, a well-functioning interbank market is also critical to
rebuilding our reserve buffers. Our efforts to improve communication and transparency by
publishing semi-annual reserve targets and FX interventions continue to be instrumental in helping
market participants gauge overall FX demand.

25.     The SBP’s framework towards liberalizing the foreign exchange regime will support
private sector development and spur foreign investment. As a first step, we have removed
onerous regulations that unduly burden banks and their clients, including certain documentary
requirements. This sets us on a broader path from ex-ante verification to risk-based ex-post
supervision of FX transactions. The SBP is also developing a roadmap for gradual foreign exchange
regime liberalization, spelling out the appropriate sequencing, including the macroeconomic,
financial stability, and other structural preconditions needed for each liberalization step (new end-
March 2027 SB).

26.      As attracting remittances is critical to Pakistan’s external stability, we are working to
address costly payment system impediments. Work towards a comprehensive assessment of such
impediments and costs and an action plan to address them has advanced (end-May 2026 SB). As
of end-March 2026, the MoF and the SBP have agreed on a mechanism to ensure that claims arising
from the remittance subsidy schemes during any given fiscal year do not exceed the budgetary
allocation. The mechanism provides for regular monitoring of budget utilization and necessary
remedial measures, including changes in the features of the incentive’s schemes, if needed, to
ensure compliance with budget allocation.

27.      Preserving financial sector stability is a key cornerstone of our program. We are
committed to vigilant monitoring and taking decisive actions to support and strengthen the sector’s
resilience.

a.    Addressing undercapitalized financial institutions.

      i.   Banking sector. One private sector bank identified as undercapitalized as at end-March
           2025 has since implemented a multi-step recapitalization plan and is now fully compliant
           with the applicable capital requirement. Going forward, we will ensure that all banks
           maintain prescribed capital buffers and take prompt supervisory action to address any
           banks that may become undercapitalized.

     ii.   Microfinance sector. We continue to monitor vulnerabilities in the microfinance banking
           sector and are addressing the negative sector-wise capital adequacy ratio, which has
           resulted from the undercapitalization of 4 of 11 banks. We are expediting our efforts to
           encourage owners and potential investors to resolve capital shortfalls and improve




70   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                              PAKISTAN


          business models. Discussions with development partners are underway to design schemes
          supporting the sector’s long-term sustainability.

b.    Upgrading the crisis management framework. We are strengthening the bank resolution
      and crisis management frameworks to align with international best practices. Following
      amendments to the legislation in October 2024, we are developing tools to implement the
      new framework with WB technical support.

c.    Reduction of Non-Performing Loans (NPLs) of the banking sector. Banks have made
      progress in implementing their plans to reduce the stock of non-performing loans (NPLs), with
      the gross NPL ratio declining to 6.1 percent as of end-2025. We continue to closely monitor
      developments and the effective execution of these plans.

d.    Effective implementation of the AML/CFT framework. Informed by the ongoing update of
      the 2023 National Risk Assessment and under robust coordination by the National AML/CFT
      authority, we will prioritize AML/CFT reforms with a focus on (i) enhancing the effectiveness of
      AML/CFT supervision of and preventive measures by designated non-financial businesses and
      professions (DNFPBs); and (ii) improving the availability and accuracy of beneficial ownership
      (BO) information, especially on the SECP’s central registry, to prevent misuse of legal entities.
      On the DNFBP sector, notably real estate agents, we will address the low levels of suspicious
      transaction reporting (STRs) through ongoing reform of the STR reporting framework and
      requiring entities to register with the goAML system. On BO transparency, SECP will continue
      to take measures to enhance the compliance rate and strengthen verification mechanisms,
      including through systematic cross referencing across databases. To prevent risks from
      Trade-based Money Laundering (TBML), the SBP published a standalone regulatory framework
      in August 2025 to help Authorized Dealers assess and monitor TBML risks in their customers,
      transactions, and services. Moreover, the FMU actively shares financial intelligence with
      relevant agencies, especially leveraging proactive outreach by customs. Going forward, we will
      enhance interagency data sharing by exploring a range of data points and policy instruments,
      including through coordination on FX reporting on import payments and customs data, to
      better monitor and mitigate the risks of TBML at both macro-trend and transactional levels.

28.      We remain committed to our long-term financial sector reform objectives and
ensuring a sound regulatory framework. A high-level interagency committee led by the Ministry
of Finance will soon finalize and disclose the government’s post-2027 financial sector strategy,
setting clear expectations for financial institutions from 2028 (end-June 2026 SB). As we integrate
virtual assets into our financial system, we will follow a structured approach that balances innovation
with strong regulatory safeguards to ensure investor protection and transparency. Following the
enactment of the Virtual Assets Act, we will develop a robust framework through comprehensive
secondary regulations, to be issued by end-June 2026 and a detailed implementation plan to ensure
effective governance, while the new Virtual Asset Regulatory Authority will finalize its coordination
arrangements with key financial safety net counterparts. Efforts to integrate virtual assets will be
aligned with broader macro financial policies, with a view to safeguarding monetary sovereignty,
preventing capital flow volatility, and maintaining financial stability.


                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       71
PAKISTAN


29.      We continue to foster a more efficient allocation of credit and limit the role of the
public sector in the financial system. As part of our overall economic development strategy we
will ensure that Exim Bank’s subsidized lending programs are in line with the better targeting plan as
already announced by Exim Bank. To limit distortions and avoid challenges for the SBP in pursuing
its mandated objectives, Exim’s lending programs will remain limited to at most 15 percent of
private sector credit, including for the phaseout of the SBP’s refinancing schemes. We continue
consultations with stakeholders to reform Development Finance Institutions (DFIs) and ensure they
support private sector investment. Finally, work continues to transfer the SBP’s stake in one financial
institution to the government.

30.     Digitalization is a cornerstone to our efforts to deepen financial penetration and
better channel capital to the private sector. Adoption of our instant payment system (Raast)
continues to gain strong momentum both in person-to-person and person-to business transactions.
The implementation of the new RTGS system (now called PRISM+) has been completed. The new
system with advanced features, especially the integrated Central Securities Depository (CDS) for
Government Securities, provides efficiency and transparency in large value payments and settlement
of securities. We aim to (i) advance further integration of payment systems with other countries to
reduce costs to consumers; and (ii) digitalize all the Federal and Provincial Government payments by
June 2027.

Energy Sector Policies

31.      Achieving energy sector viability is key to maintaining macroeconomic stability. We
commit to continuing to do so through timely tariff increases that recover costs and prevent a
recurrence of circular debt. The implementation of these necessary adjustments in the context of
recent shocks to global energy markets is critical to ensuring the sector’s viability and broader
macroeconomic stability without unduly burdening the budget. We commit to maintaining
progressive power and gas tariff structures. We are enhancing sectoral viability through our
structural reform agenda, which aims to reduce costs by improving efficiency. We have established
an institutional arrangement in the form of a dedicated National Integrated Energy Plan (IEP)
Secretariat to lead the development of the IEP, targeted for completion by April 2027. The
development process is inclusive and consultative, engaging all key stakeholders, including
provincial governments, through thematic working groups. Overall guidance is provided by a
high-level Steering Committee, co-chaired by the Ministers for Power and Petroleum, with decision-
making done through the Cabinet Committee on Energy. This will, among other things, enable
stakeholders to make better-informed decisions on supply and demand across the energy sector
value chain.

32.     We commit to further reduce power CD flow, as reflected in our lower FY27 CD flow
target, and achieving net zero power CD flow in FY26 and FY27. Our strategy will be detailed in
our FY27 CD Management Plan (CDMP), to be adopted by cabinet by the end of July 2026, and will
entail:




72   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                               PAKISTAN


a.   Timely electricity tariff adjustments consistent with cost recovery that remain
     progressive, with a focus remaining on protecting vulnerable consumers. This includes
     NEPRA’s continued timely automatic notifications of quarterly tariff adjustments (QTAs) and
     monthly fuel charges adjustments (FCAs), as well as the full implementation of the January
     2027 annual rebasing (new January 15, 2027 SB), all of which will continue in the context of
     recent global energy market volatility.

b.   Targeted budget allocations for power subsidies. Following the implementation of the CD
     stock reduction operation in FY26 and recognizing ongoing improvements in operational
     efficiency and performance, the FY27 budget will include a subsidy limited to, at most,
     Rs 830 billion, or 0.6 percent of GDP. The subsidy will cover (i) the projected tariff differential
     for DISCOs and KE; (ii) current and arrears payments of FATA; (iii) agricultural tubewells; and
     (iv) CD stock payments to counterbalance anticipated CD flow, which continues to be targeted
     at a lower level following the CD stock operation.

c.   Completion of arrangements with IPPs and Karachi Electric (KE). Settlement with several
     IPPs with whom penalty payments on arrears were to be waived as part of the broader CD
     stock reduction operation remains incomplete, with CD continuing to accumulate as a result.
     We will finalize arrangements with all IPPs by end-June 2026. Likewise, we will seek to resolve
     a dispute with KE currently under litigation, which has resulted in significant nonpayment and
     arrears, by the end of September 2026.

We anticipate that these steps will minimize CD flow, allowing a lower end-FY27 gross CD flow
target of Rs 300 billion, and remain committed to reducing gross CD flow to zero by FY31.

33.   We continue to move forward with fundamental cost-reducing power sector reforms.
Our power agenda includes:

a.   Improving distribution. Private sector participation in DISCO management will improve
     performance, efficiency, and governance and address power sector CD drivers, helping to
     mitigate the need for higher tariffs. The first batch of privatization (IESCO, GEPCO, and FESCO)
     has been delayed following feedback from market sounding exercises; investor concerns have
     been addressed and we are moving forward, anticipating finalization by early 2027. In parallel,
     we are moving forward with the private sector participation process for the second batch of
     DISCOs (HESCO and SEPCO), for which conditions precedent – in line with World Bank
     recommendations and including outstanding subsidy claims; outstanding balances with the
     government, other DISCOs, and other entities; and other balance sheet issues – will be
     completed by the end of December 2026 (end-December 2026 SB). We are also working
     with the remaining DISCOs in a similar fashion to prepare their balance sheets for private
     sector participation.

b.   Improving the transmission system. The appointment of a CEO to the Independent System
     and Market Operator (ISMO) is underway, as are efforts to finalize staffing arrangements. The
     incorporation and legal formation of the Energy Infrastructure and Development Management


                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND        73
PAKISTAN


     Company (EIDMC) have been completed, and its leadership selection process has been
     completed. The National Grid Company (NGC) is operational and is undergoing a review of its
     processes in the context of its new role. Taken together, this restructuring of the transmission
     network management aims to improve the operations and maintenance, and ultimately the
     functioning, of Pakistan’s electricity transmission network via ongoing and anticipated new
     projects, with the support of the World Bank Group and Asian Development Bank.

c.   Privatizing inefficient generation companies (GENCOs). We are working closely with the
     Privatization Commission to assess the viability of privatizing two targeted GENCOs (Nandipur
     and Guddu) (¶36.e). In the event that privatization does not prove feasible, we will work to
     bring relevant companies under one entity to reduce redundancies, make necessary
     improvements, and enhance operations.

d.   Completing the transition to a competitive electricity market. NEPRA issued wheeling
     auction framework guidelines in January 2026; this will enable auctions under the auspices of
     the Competitive Trading and Bilateral Contract Market (CTBCM). The first wheeling auction, for
     200 MW, will take place by end-June 2026. Once operational, the new market will allow bulk
     power consumers greater choice over their electricity supplier. The NEPRA-determined
     wheeling charge will be set such that up to 800 MW will be allowed in the market until 2031 as
     a transition measure, with scope for adjustment based on market response, system conditions,
     and policy direction, to ensure that sufficient capacity remains on the grid.

e.   Facilitating the move to renewable energy and rationalizing capacity relative to demand
     while securing grid sustainability. The publication of the revised 2025-35 Integrated System
     Plan, combining capacity (the former IGCEP) and transmission (TSEP) planning and aiming to
     reduce excess capacity and deliver significant savings, has been delayed as public hearings
     have been more extensive than expected. The Plan will be published by the end of May 2026.
     At the same time, we will apply the recently adopted net billing regulation to new consumers
     to better balance solar and grid demand, in line with international practice.

f.   Enhancing the Power Planning and Monitoring Company (PPMC). We have restructured
     the PPMC as a technical arm of the Power Division. It supports policy, regulatory, and tariff
     affairs; sector reforms; privatization; CD management; and integrated power and energy
     planning. We will further strengthen the human resources and secure a sustainable fee-based
     financing mechanism for PPMC to ensure its financial sustainability and resource adequacy so
     that it can effectively support the planning, monitoring, and reform implementation of the
     power sector and the energy sector more broadly, by end-December 2026. The financing
     mechanism will be developed and implemented consistent with the applicable legal and
     regulatory processes, including NEPRA’s oversight on cost recovery.




74   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                PAKISTAN


34.    Our gas sector strategy hinges on continued timely tariff adjustment and improved CD
data and monitoring to preserve sectoral stability and viability.

a.    We are navigating the recent disruptions in global gas markets through necessary price
      adjustments, planned improvements in RLNG management, stakeholder coordination, data
      monitoring, and gas loss prevention in order to maintain both sectoral viability and supplies.
      This includes our decision in March 2026 to increase consumer diesel and petrol prices by
      20 percent following parallel spikes in global prices, as well as efforts underway with RLNG
      suppliers to facilitate sufficient supply.

b.    We will continue to notify semiannual gas tariff adjustments in line with cost recovery, as
      determined by OGRA (July 1, 2026 and February 15, 2027 new SBs), while preserving tariff
      structure progressivity, protecting vulnerable household consumers, and fully reflecting the
      cost of imported RLNG diverted to domestic sector. We will also continue working to address
      RLNG surplus issues in the gas sector, including by continuing to seek to rationalize RLNG
      supplies while remaining within the parameters of existing contracts and through enhanced
      coordination and data-sharing with power sector counterparts under the newly-established
      integrated energy planning framework and Cabinet Committee on Energy.

c.    We have developed a comprehensive dashboard featuring sub-components and drivers of CD.
      We have initiated a quarterly gas CD flow reporting system (also benefiting from improved
      data management and projection capacity) and have developed a CD Management Plan
      (CDMP) which we expect to roll out in FY27 after seeking necessary approvals. There will be a
      dedicated CD settlement wing/office to oversee the implementation of the CDMP. To that
      end, we will continue to provide quarterly gas CD data within two months of close of a
      quarter, along with monthly gas consumption and revenue data, to the Fund and other
      development partners with a six-week lag to the close of relevant billing month.

d.    We are reducing CD pressures and unaccounted for gas (UFG) by improving gas system
      tracking and enforcement, improving ageing infrastructure (about 4,000 kilometers of
      transmission lines were upgraded last year and we will achieve the same this year, subject to
      OGRA determinations), and reviewing how Suis are benchmarked against losses.

35.     We see an urgency to accelerate potential growth to ensure faster convergence of per
capita income to regional and emerging market peers. This requires that our efforts focus on
broad-based structural reforms—improving governance, reducing inefficiencies, boosting
productivity and supporting private sector development—the only way to foster long-term growth
without undermining macroeconomic stability and fiscal sustainability.

36.       Advancing our SOE reform agenda is key to scaling back the state’s footprint and
improving services. We are progressing toward filling remaining gaps in our SOE governance
framework and, in parallel, implementing the framework to improve SOE’s viability, reduce fiscal
liabilities and the role of the state in commercial enterprises, and improve services for Pakistanis.
This includes:


                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND          75
PAKISTAN


a.   Ensuring coverage of all SOEs under the SOE governance framework. We have sent
     6 amendments to SOE-dedicated laws to parliament for approval, to fully align them with SOE
     Act provisions, and are making progress on amending the remaining three SOE- with
     dedicated laws (end-August 2026 SB).

b.   Adopting appropriate governance mechanisms and safeguards for the Sovereign Wealth
     Fund (SWF) and its SOEs. We have finalized, with Fund staff consultation, amendments to the
     SWF Law to bring it into line with international standards for public asset management and
     SOE holding companies and will submit to Parliament soon . These amendments, once
     adopted by Parliament (end-March 2026 SB), will achieve the objectives outlined below, and
     the execution of the SWF Act and any other action aimed at preparing the SWF for its
     operation will only be carried out after these amendments become law.

            i.   Clarify the legal nature and mandate of the SWF. Legally define the SWF as a SOE,
                 subject it to the SOE Act (adopting specific provisions to ensure the SWF’s
                 governance structures correspond with a holding entity’s nature and mandate), and
                 narrow its mandate to (i) holding and managing SOEs on behalf of the state and
                 creating value through their operational and financial improvement; and (ii) attracting
                 foreign direct investment by facilitating and mobilizing co-investment in strategic
                 commercial ventures that generate financial returns in line with the SWF’s Investment
                 Mandate.

           ii.   Divestments and procurement. Requiring that privatization or sales of assets and its
                 procurement processes are conducted based on rules and policies adopted and
                 published by the SWF’s Board that are in line with international standards and best
                 practices and ensure open, competitive, transparent and non-discriminatory
                 procedures, requiring minimum disclosure requirements for each stage of the
                 process, including on beneficial ownership.

        iii.     SWF governance. Establish that the appointment of the SWF’s Board and Advisory
                 Committee’s members is done through transparent, merit-based, and participatory
                 processes to safeguard their professionalism and independence from undue public
                 and private influence, including by introducing effective cooling off periods for
                 stronger independence.

        iv.      Governance of SWF-SOEs. Ensure that SWF-owned SOEs remain under the same
                 high-quality governance structures and accountability standards as all other SOEs,
                 including by amending section 50 of the SWF Act to explicitly establish that
                 SWF-owned SOEs are subject to the SOE Act and SOE Policy and any necessary
                 additional amendments to regulate and operationalize the SWF’s SOE ownership
                 functions with SWF being owner/holding entity with appropriate oversight systems as
                 agreed with Fund staff.




76   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                 PAKISTAN


            v.    Fiscal safeguards. Ensure that appropriate fiscal safeguards are in place, including by
                  requiring that all revenues from SWF and sub-funds operations will be provided
                  directly to the government and not retained by the SWF and that any funds required
                  for SWF investments shall be allocated by the Federal Government through the
                  budgetary process under Public Financial Management Act. In addition, by
                  prohibiting the SWF to (i) incur debt or borrowing in any way; (ii) provide guarantees
                  or collateral, including over SWF-SOEs shares or assets; (iii) lend to public or private
                  entities, or any persons, foreign or domestic; (iv) participate in PPPs; (v) acquire
                  financial assets or instruments of any kind; or (vi) receive any contributions from the
                  central bank, SOEs or any other public body.

c.      Adhering to the SOE governance framework. We are planning to carry out the federal
        rightsizing committee’s review of the purpose and rationale of various SOEs via a phased
        process and have included ten SOEs in the first phase. Of the ten, four are in the process of
        being privatized, another three are in the process of being wound up, and the last three are
        marked to enter the due diligence process, which we will complete by end-July 2026. We will
        complete the review of all SOEs by end-June 2027. We have finalized, working with the Asian
        Development Bank (ADB), guidance notes on business plans and statements of corporate
        intent, which will be published by end-May 2026. As of March 2026, thirty-four SOEs have now
        adopted and published business plans and statements of corporate intent. Thirty-nine SOEs
        have also issued IFRS-compliant financial statements. We will continue to work with SOEs to
        enhance the quality of reporting, including by providing additional training where needed. We
        will maintain international IFRS standards for all SOEs, with exceptions only for individual cases
        granted by the SECP/Cabinet Committee on SOEs. We have adopted and published a
        preliminary public service obligation (PSO) framework, which will guide the pilot incorporation
        of PSO agreements for the seven largest PSOs into the FY27 budget (SB end-June 2026).
        Over the course of FY27, we will further refine the guidelines and incorporate an additional
        three PSOs, including the NHA, into the FY28 budget. Restructuring plans for both PR and the
        NHA have been completed and published.1 Nearly all commercial SOEs now have majority-
        independent boards, aside from those that do not yet fall under the SOE Act or are at
        advanced stages of the privatization process. Independent boards will be appointed for the
        former once their statutory laws are brought into line with the SOE Act. Guidelines for the
        appointment of directors have been finalized and published.2

d.      Fully operationalizing the CMU. Our electronic database is now fully operational and online.
        We are exploring options for further automation of data integration; moreover, our next
        biannual report will be drawn from the portal. We will continue to deepen our reports’ analysis
        of SOE business plans and performance with subsequent reports.

e.    Advancing the privatization agenda. Some progress has been made toward privatizing the
      27 entities on the privatization list. Agreement on PIA privatization was signed in January 2026
      and on First Women’s Bank in October 2025. The first batch of DISCO private sector
      participation has been delayed as further steps are taken to address market/investor concerns,
      although we are moving forward with the second batch in parallel. We remain focused on a
      joint venture structure for the privatization of the Roosevelt Hotel, with the hiring of a new
      financial advisor expected to be completed by end-April 2026. We are re-initiating the
      privatization process for HBFC following an unsuccessful bid in December 2025. We have
      started work for a long-term concession for the Islamabad airport, and are currently hiring a
      financial advisor.

37.      In order to implement a shift towards an export led and private sector driven
economy, we are taking ambitious steps to scale back government intervention in commodity
markets and enable private sector initiative. Amidst extreme uncertainty and food price volatility,
this will reduce the risk of shortages in staple food commodities. Our interim wheat policy for the
2025/26 Rabi season, adopted in November 2025, has successfully guided expectations to help
boost area under cultivation to a multi-year high. Procurement for building of strategic reserves will
be executed through contracted private sector operators and procurement prices will be aligned
with international market prices. In the meantime, federal and provincial governments, through
mutual consultation, are advancing work towards a long-term wheat policy, to be adopted by end-
May 2026, with the objective of removing structural impediments to price discovery and market
development, including any barriers for trade between provinces. We are also advancing work
towards the full liberalization of the sugar sector and have shared draft recommendations for a
national sugar policy with provincial governments. A draft policy will be formulated by end-March
and the new national policy will be agreed by federal and provincial governments and adopted by
the federal cabinet by end-June 2026 (end-June 2026 SB).

38.    We will strengthen our institutional capacities and take further measures to fight
corruption to support inclusive growth and provide a level playing field for businesses and
investments.

a.   Implementation of the Governance and Corruption Diagnostic (GCD). We have established
     three committees to monitor progress with the recently published Economic Governance
     Reform (EGR) plan to implement the priority recommendations of the GCD report. We will
     prepare progress reports on a six-month basis to track implementation and will publish them
     on the website of the Ministry of Finance. We are also organizing a policy dialogue (April
     2026) to discuss institutional and structural implementation issues, design of performance
     indicators, common challenges and cross-cutting issues, public monitoring, reporting, and
     capacity development. We will invite development partners, civil society organizations and
     other stakeholders to participate in this process. On the basis of these discussions and in close
     consultation with key stakeholders, we will develop and publish in our semi-annual report
     outcome-based updates on progress. These updates will serve as a backdrop to a second




78   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                    PAKISTAN


      policy dialogue in July 2026, which will take stock of the six-month implementation of the EGR
      plan.

b.    UNCAC Review Report. In line with our commitment to transparency and accountability, we
      informed the UNODC Secretariat of the publication of UNCAC Review Report by the Ministry
      of Law and Justice. The report is now also available on the UNODC’s website.3

c.    Corruption Risk Assessment. Tasked by the Anti-Corruption and AML/CFT Committee,
      constituted by the Prime Minister of Pakistan and chaired by the Minister for Law and Justice,
      the National Accountability Bureau (NAB) has been designated to lead the development of an
      action plan to mitigate corruption vulnerabilities in the top ten departments of the
      Government identified with highest corruption risks (proposed revised end-October 2026
      SB). To guide the development of the plan, in consultation and agreement with IMF staff, by
      end-June 2026, the Anti-Corruption and AML/CFT Committee will develop and publish a
      methodology for assessing and prioritizing agency level corruption risks, and protocols for
      conducting the risk assessment, reporting and reviewing results of the analysis, and defining
      the plan to reduce corruption risks in identified agencies. Methodological alignment as a
      preliminary step to the corruption risk assessment is critical to ensure its comprehensiveness
      and effective use of all relevant information sources. The methodology should lay out the
      assessment criteria, making use of relevant information held by agencies and ministries across
      government, including the NAB, Auditor General, the Competition Committee, the FBR, and
      the FIA, and consider: (i) the value of money at risk due to corruption vulnerabilities pertaining
      to the agency’s functions and budget; (ii) typologies of corruption in the assessed agency; (iii)
      the existence of structural weaknesses that give rise to corruption vulnerabilities; (iv)
      information on the frequency of corruption, including past and on-going corruption cases.

d.    Provincial Anti-Corruption Establishments (PACEs). We will continue enhancing capacities
      of PACEs to conduct financial investigations in relation to corruption at the provincial level. In
      line with the AML Act and the National Fiscal Pact, the relevant federal notification process
      initiated by the Financial Monitoring Unit (FMU) will be issued by end-December 2026,
      designating the PACEs to investigate money laundering related to corruption offenses within
      their jurisdiction, and to request and receive financial intelligence from the FMU as an
      investigating agency.

e.     Asset Declaration of High-Level Public Officials. To support the online publication of asset
       declarations of high-level federal civil servants (end-December 2026 SB), the Establishment
       Division revised the Civil Servant (Conduct) Rules requiring: (i) centralized digital submission
       and collection of asset declarations; (ii) risked-based verification; and (iii) disclosure of
       declarations with limited restrictions on confidential personal information. To this end, the
       Establishment Division will revise the declaration form to specify restrictions on confidential
