      personal information by end-May 2026, and in coordination with the FBR, will develop a
      framework for risk-based verifications by end-June 2027. The FBR will develop a digital
      platform for the submission of asset declarations by end July 2026 to facilitate the
      implementation of the reform.

f.    Banks’ Access to Asset Declarations for AML/CFT Purposes. The SBP, FBR, and FMU
      continue to support banks’ access to asset declarations of high-level federal public officials
      (BPS17-22). The FBR has issued a notification to expand banks’ access to cover asset
      declarations of any officer of the federal or provincial governments or autonomous bodies,
      corporations, and companies owned by such governments. To enhance banks’ awareness, by
      June 2026, we will publish access statistics on FBR’s website.

g.    Independence and operation of NAB. As the leading anti-corruption agency, NAB’s
      institutional independence plays a key role in building public trust and institutional credibility.
      We will submit to parliament amendments to the NAB ordinance to ensure NAB’s autonomy
      and transparency through a robust selection process of senior management and publication
      of operating rules and statistics (new end-January 2027 SB). To this end, we will review and
      improve the appointment process of the NAB chairman. Under the guidance of the Anti-
      Corruption and AML/CFT Committee, proposed amendments to the NAB Ordinance will be
      finalized and submitted to Parliament to: (i) adopt pre-determined qualification criteria (e.g.,
      years of experience, integrity standards); (ii) establish a merit-based, open and competitive
      selection process; and (iii) designate a multi-sectoral stakeholder committee (with
      representatives from the government, opposition, judiciary, civil service, academia, and civil
      society) to conduct an open, rules-based rigorous and transparent recruitment. We will also
      finalize and publish NAB’s investigation and prosecution rules, as well as annual statistics
      regarding the investigation, prosecution and conviction of corruption offences on NAB’s
      website.

39.     We continue to reduce barriers to international trade, including through the National
Tariff and Auto Policies:

a.   Implementing the new National Tariff Policy (NTP). We are continuing to implement the
     2025-30 NTP, with the second phase of duty reductions to be legislated in the FY27 Finance
     Act, which is expected to considerably reduce the weighted average tariff, consistent with the
     agreed reductions under the program (Table 3).

b.   Liberalizing auto sector imports. A new auto sector policy is in the advanced stages, and will
     be shared with the Fund by end-April 2026, ahead of its approval by the Cabinet. Through its
     progressive elimination of all ACDs and RDs, and substantial reduction in CD rates by FY30, it
     will be consistent with the agreed overall reduction in the weighted average tariff (Table 3).
     The Motor Vehicle Development Act, which gives the Engineering Development Board a
     statutory basis to enforce the new regime of environmental and safety standards for locally
     manufactured and imported vehicles, has been submitted to the parliament and is expected to
     be approved by the National Assembly before end-June 2026. With commercial importation


80   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                          PAKISTAN


     now legalized, we have abolished the personal baggage scheme and tightened criteria for the
     gift and transfer of residence schemes for used vehicle imports, to avoid misuse of these
     schemes.

c.   Addressing non-tariff barriers (NTBs). Our stock-take of the existing Export Policy Order
     and Import Policy Order has mapped 2,662 NTBs for review, with restrictions affecting 76 HS
     codes identified for removal by end-May, 2026. The remaining NTBs will be reviewed in
     batches, and presented to the Cabinet Committee on Regulatory Reform, for removal or
     simplification by end-September, 2026.

40.     As part of our ongoing efforts to improve efficiency and provide a level playing field
for investment, the government will refrain from providing any new fiscal incentives, such as
tax breaks or subsidies (including on bank credit).

a.   Special Economic Zones (SEZs), Export Processing Zones (EPZs) Special Technology Zones
     (STZs). Building on our comprehensive plan to fully phase out all current SEZ and EPZ
     incentives by 2035, we are preparing a concept note defining the scope, objectives, and
     expected outcomes of legislative amendments to the SEZ Act (end-June 2026 SB). For STZs,
     building on the comprehensive plan to phase out their incentives by 2035, we will prepare a
     concept note on the scope, objectives and expected outcomes of legislative amendments to
     the STZA Act by October 2026. By June 2027, we will enact amendments to: (i) the SEZ Act and
     phase-out existing fiscal incentives to SEZs (consistent with the FY26 Finance Bill) and shift
     from profit- based to cost-based incentives as per the agreed phase-out plan, and discontinue
     the rights and responsibilities of the BOA, BOI, and existing SEZ Authorities in granting tax
     incentives ; and (ii) the STZA Act to phase-out all existing fiscal incentives to STZs by 2035
     (new end-June 2027 SB). For EPZs, we have drafted amendments to prohibit sales from EPZs
     to the domestic market, which will be implemented upon approval by the Cabinet by
     September 2026. We remain committed to not introducing new zones and phasing out
     existing ones over time, with a view to levelling the playing field for investment and
     strengthening the business environment nationally.

b.   Regulatory Reforms to enhance the Business Climate. We have advanced our regulatory
     reform agenda through the Asaan Karobar (AK) Act, which was enacted in November 2025.
     The Rules for operationalization of the AK Technical Unit (Regulatory Quality Control Office)
     are being drafted, and approval by the Federal cabinet is expected by end-June 2026. The AK
     technical unit will complete a comprehensive review of existing regulations, which will be
     streamlined through a series of omnibus packages over a two-year horizon. The first omnibus
     package, comprising Federal RLCOs, would be presented to the Cabinet Committee on
     Regulatory Reform by end-September 2026, with parliamentary approval anticipated by end-
     December 2026. We will also establish the new Pakistan Regulatory Registry as a
     comprehensive and legally authoritative source on business regulations, starting with Federal
     Government and ICT regulations (new end-June 2027 SB), and later extending to all
     provincial regulations. The BOI is coordinating with provincial governments to ensure passage
     of provincial AK Acts, which would extend regulatory streamlining to the provincial level.

PAKISTAN


c.   Reforms to the Companies Act. Following the Securities and Exchange Commission of
     Pakistan (SECP) proposal, Cabinet will approve and submit to parliament amendments to the
     Companies Act (end-June 2026 SB) to reduce the regulatory burden and transaction costs for
     companies and improve corporate governance and transparency for both listed and unlisted
     companies, including, among others, better clarifying directors’ duties and liabilities, enabling
     stock-exchanges in monitoring corporate governance compliance, enhanced disclosure
     requirements for related party transactions, improving independence requirements for
     auditors, and strengthening protections for minority shareholders.

41.   We continue to advance our efforts to improve the timeliness, quality and coverage of
macroeconomic data:

a.   Major Surveys and Producer Price Index (PPI). The major surveys conducted in FY25 have
     been completed, and the PBS published results of the Labor Force Survey and Household
     Integrated Economic Survey in December and early January, respectively. An initiation survey
     for the new PPI (Industrial) will be completed by June 2026, which will create a basis for full
     collection of monthly prices starting in October, and the first internal dissemination to key
     stakeholders in January 2027. Data collection for three upcoming major surveys (the Census of
     Manufacturing Industries, the survey of Small and Household Manufacturing Industries, and
     the Family Budget Survey), will commence in the coming months, with publication of the CMI
     and FBS results expected late in FY27, and the SHMI in early FY28.

b.   Government Finance Statistics. The GFS mappings developed for GFSM 2014 reporting have
     been introduced into the IFMIS system, which will allow reports based on GFSM 2014 to be
     generated once ongoing system upgrades are completed by end-August 2026. We will
     continue our work on reconciling SOE accrual accounts with government’s modified cash basis
     accounts. We will also seek to broaden the coverage of the above-the-line statistics to
     gradually include more extrabudgetary units with the objective of minimizing statistical
     discrepancy in government accounts.

c.   Trade Statistics. The PBS has prepared a full set of revised quarterly and annual import
     statistics for 2020-25, reflecting a full reconciliation of data collected by the Pakistan Single
     Window and PRAL, following issues previously identified with the source data. PBS will prepare
     revised monthly import statistics for 2020-25 for public dissemination by end-August 2026,
     alongside appropriate explanations. Procedures for ongoing import data collection are also
     being strengthened. The PBS is working closely with PSW to develop standardized data
     extraction criteria and data definitions to ensure trade data remains consistent across all
     stakeholders; this will be completed by end-May 2026. Further to that, a regular consultative
     mechanism is being put in place to ensure that any modifications to the trade data are
     appropriately reflected in PBS data reported to internal and external stakeholders.

Building Climate Resilience

42.      Building climate change resilience remains a priority for Pakistan, one of the world’s
most climate-vulnerable countries. This was evidenced during the catastrophic 2022 floods and
the severe 2025 floods. Policies supported by the Resilience and Stability Facility (RSF), in line with
our National Adaptation Plan and commitments under our Nationally Determined Contributions, are
helping to build resilience. The RSF is (i) mainstreaming climate issues into budget and investment
planning; (ii) improving water system resilience and disaster response financing coordination;
(iii) enhancing the enabling environment for green investments by strengthening Pakistan’s climate
information architecture; (iv) promoting green mobility and transport decarbonization; and
(v) aligning energy sector reforms with our national climate change commitments.

43.     Both RMs due under the second RSF review have been implemented. The SBP issued
guidelines for the management of climate-related financial risks on December 26, 2025 (RM7).
Based on Pakistan’s recently-adopted green taxonomy, the SECP issued guidelines to enable listed
companies to disclose climate-related risks and opportunities on December 11, 2025 (RM8). Taken
together, these measures will strengthen our climate information architecture and management of
climate-related financial risk, thus helping to finance private sector climate finance.

44.    Progress toward future RMs is ongoing.

a.    We are moving toward establishing, with Fund support, an implementation framework for
      coordinating federal and provincial disaster risk financing needs in the context of the National
      Disaster Risk Financing Strategy (RM6, end-August 2026).

b.    We are making progress toward establishing a comprehensive system for identifying and
      prioritizing climate-relevant spending. In the near future, we will increase the climate
      weighting in our Public Investment Procedures and Parameters methodology to at least
      30 percent for infrastructure projects; develop explicit protocols for scoring projects against
      criteria; and publish the selection process and distribution of scores for new projects entering
      the PSDP (RM1, end-August 2026).

c.    We are working toward improving Pakistan’s water system resilience: the digital e-Abiana
      irrigation service charge system, already in place in Punjab, is targeted for development and
      rollout in Sindh, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, and Balochistan (RM4, end-August 2027). We are also
      working toward establishing irrigation water tariff adjustment mechanisms consistent with
      operations and maintenance cost recovery in Punjab and Sindh (RM5, end-February 2027).
      Provinces will seek assistance from World Bank expertise as necessary to ensure completion of
      these objectives.

d.    We are proceeding with our planned electricity subsidy reform (RM12, end-January 2027).
      This will replace the budgeted tariff differential subsidy and cross-subsidy system with a
      targeted budgeted subsidy framework for low-income consumers via BISP. We are working
      closely with the World Bank to link electricity consumers to the NSER database; we will strive



                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       83
PAKISTAN


     to complete the linking, along with validity checks by end-November 2026, after which we will
     determine eligibility criteria. In parallel, we are launching a communications drive, for which
     we will hire an external firm by end-May 2026 and are developing a payments mechanism for
     the subsidy. We notified in December 2025 the PPRA’s adoption of new regulations
     mandating MEPS-compliant procurement and are now working with the provincial
     governments to adopt this at the provincial level. We are also working on a tracking system to
     measure progress toward MEPS compliance for five select electronic appliances at the product
     level, which will be in place by end-December 2026. (RM13, end-June 2027).

Financing and Program Monitoring under the EFF and RSF

45.      We have secured adequate financing from our international partners to support our
economic reform program and continue rebuilding our external buffers. Based on existing
financing commitments from bilateral and multilateral partners, our program is fully financed for the
next 12 months. As committed at the outset of the EFF, our bilateral partners will also continue
rolling over short-term claims (including loans, swaps and deposits) for the duration of the program.

46.     Implementation of policies under the program will be monitored through semiannual
reviews, with semiannual performance criteria and continuous performance criteria, as set out
in our MEFPs dated September 11, 2024, April 24, 2025, and November 20, 2025, and along with this
MEFP. The attached Technical Memorandum of Understanding (TMU) defines the quantitative
performance criteria, continuous performance criteria, indicative targets, and structural benchmarks
under the program. Completion of the fourth review scheduled for September 2026 will require
observance of the quantitative performance criteria for end-June 2026, as set out in Table 1, along
with continuous PCs and ITs. The prior actions and structural benchmarks are set out in Table 2.
Standing commitments are listed in Table 3. Progress in the implementation of policies under the
RSF will be monitored through RMs, which are detailed in Table 4, and semiannual reviews are to
occur concurrently with EFF reviews.

  22      The incremental pricing plan will not allow differentiated treatment to specific industries or sectors. A review of the Power                              2nd            33.c
          incremental price and scheme will automatically be triggered to incorporate any additional marginal cost if growth
          of incremental consumption of the industrial and agriculture sectors in aggregate exceeds 25 percent above the
          baseline in a given month. Semi-annual reviews of the scheme will ensure continued cost revenue alignment. Any
          losses due to the scheme’s failure of revenues to keep up with tariffs will be borne fully by higher tariffs on industry
          and agriculture. Only positive FCAs will be applicable to incremental consumption. The scheme will expire three
          years from its inception date, with no possibility of rollover, and will be terminated immediately if it requires
          upward tariff adjustment in two consecutive reviews.

         Attachment II. Technical Memorandum of Understanding
1.       This Technical Memorandum of Understanding (TMU) sets out the understanding
between the Pakistani authorities and the IMF staff regarding: (i) the definitions of quantitative
performance criteria (and their adjustment mechanisms), indicative targets, and—if needed—prior
actions and structural benchmarks in Section A below; and (ii) the respective reporting
requirements used to monitor developments—for the economic program under the Extended Fund
Facility (EFF)—as described in the authorities’ latest

2.      For purposes of monitoring under the program, all assets and liabilities as well as debt
contracted, denominated in Special Drawing Rights (SDRs) or in currencies other than the U.S. dollar,
are converted into U.S. dollars at the program exchange rates. The program exchange rate of the
Pakistani rupee to the U.S. dollar is set at 278.3412 rupee per one U.S. dollar. The corresponding
cross exchange rates for other foreign currencies are provided in Text Table 1.


DEFINITION OF PERFORMANCE CRITERIA AND INDICATIVE TARGETS
A. Overview

3.     The program sets performance criteria and indicative targets for defined test dates
(MEFP Table 1) as well as defines continuous performance criteria that apply throughout the
program period. The program sets the following performance criteria:
•    Performance Criteria.
     o   Floor on the net international reserves (NIR) of the State Bank of Pakistan (SBP) (millions
         of U.S. dollars);
     o   Ceiling on the net domestic assets (NDA) of the SBP (stock, billions of Pakistani rupees);
     o   Ceiling on SBP’s stock of net foreign currency swap/forward position (millions of U.S. dollars);
     o   Ceiling on the general government primary budget deficit excluding grants (cumulative flows,
         billions of Pakistani rupees);
     o   Ceiling on the amount of government guarantees (stock, billions of Pakistani rupees);
     o   Floor on targeted cash transfers spending (BISP) (cumulative, billions of Pakistani rupees); and
     o   Floor on the number of new tax returns (cumulative, thousands).



92   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                  PAKISTAN


•   Continuous Performance Criteria.
    o   No new flow of SBP’s credit to general government;
    o   Zero ceiling on the accumulation of external public payment arrears by the general
        government; and
    o   Other (see ¶25).
•   Indicative Targets.
    o   Floor on general government budgetary health and education spending (cumulative, billions
        of Pakistani rupees);
    o   Floor on net tax revenues collected by the Federal Board of Revenue (FBR) (cumulative, billions
        of Pakistani rupees);
    o   Ceiling on net accumulation of tax refund arrears (flow, billions of Pakistani rupees);
    o   Ceiling on power sector payment arrears (flow, billions of Pakistani rupees);
    o   Floor on the weighted average time-to-maturity of the domestic debt securities stock in local
        currency, including T-bills, PIBs, and Sukuks (years);
    o   Ceiling on the aggregate provincial governments’ primary budget (cumulative, billions of
        Pakistani rupees);
    o   Floor on the consolidated net tax revenues collected by provincial revenue authorities
        (cumulative, billions of Pakistani rupees); and
    o   Floor on the income tax revenue collected by the FBR from retailers (cumulative, billions of
        Pakistani rupees).

B. Performance Criteria

B.1. Floor on the Net International Reserves (NIR) of the SBP
Definition

4.      The general government is defined as the central (federal) government and local
(provincial) governments, excluding state-owned enterprises. The definition of the general
government includes any new funds, or other special budgetary or extra-budgetary entities, that
may be created during the program period to carry out operations of a fiscal nature as defined in
the IMF’s Manual on Government Finance Statistics 2014. The authorities will inform IMF staff on the
creation of any such entities without delay.

5.      Net international reserves (stock) of the SBP are defined as the U.S. dollar value of the
difference between usable gross international reserve assets and reserve-related liabilities, evaluated
at program exchange rates.

6.      Usable gross international reserves of the SBP are readily available claims on
nonresidents denominated in foreign convertible currencies and controlled by the monetary
authorities for meeting balance of payments financing needs, for intervention in exchange markets
to affect the currency exchange rate, and for other related purposes.


                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND         93
PAKISTAN


Gross official reserves include: (i) holdings of foreign currencies; (ii) holdings of SDRs; (iii) the reserve
position in the IMF; and (iv) holdings of fixed and variable income instruments. Excluded from usable
reserves, inter alia, unless there is also a reserve-related liability associated with it, are: (i) claims on
residents; (ii) assets in nonconvertible currencies; (iii) precious metals; (iv) illiquid assets; (v) assets
that are pledged or collateralized; (vi) any reserve assets that are not readily available for
intervention in the foreign exchange market because of lack of quality or liquidity that limits
marketability at the book price; and (vii) balances held at foreign branches of non-investment rated
domestic banks.

7.        Reserve-related liabilities of the SBP include all foreign exchange liabilities to residents
(except general government) or nonresidents, including: (i) foreign currency liabilities, excluding
liabilities to the general government, with remaining maturity of one year or less; (ii) any foreign
exchange liabilities of SBP and general government arising from derivatives positions (such as
futures, forwards, swaps, and options) on a net outstanding basis (defined as the long position
minus the short position); (iii) outstanding IMF credits to Pakistan; and (iv) foreign exchange
deposits with the SBP of foreign governments, foreign central banks, foreign deposit money banks
(excluding regulatory capital deposits of foreign banks with the SBP), international organizations,
and foreign nonbank financial institutions, as well as domestic financial institutions (excluding
regulatory capital deposits of domestic financial institutions with the SBP). The reserve-related
liabilities of the SBP exclude SDR allocations and accrued interest on reserve-related liabilities.

8.       Aggregate net position in the foreign exchange derivatives is defined as the aggregate
net positions in forwards and futures in foreign currencies of the SBP and general government
vis-à-vis the domestic currency (including the forward leg of currency swaps).

9.      Gross sale of foreign exchange includes outright and swap sales of foreign exchange by
the SBP to banks in the foreign exchange interbank market by using foreign exchange market
intervention. The swap sale of foreign exchange and maturities of the forward transactions will be
measured on a net daily basis.

10.     Net purchase of foreign exchange is defined as outright and swap purchases of foreign
exchange minus outright and swap sales of foreign exchange by the SBP from/to banks in the
foreign exchange market by using foreign exchange market intervention. The net purchase of
foreign exchange will be measured on a net daily basis.

Adjustment Mechanism

11.     The floor on NIR will be adjusted upward (downward) by the cumulative excess (shortfall) in:

a.    cash inflows usable for the financing of the government budget from multilateral and
      bilateral creditors, commercial borrowing, and bond issuance relative to the projected
      inflows (Table 1). Cumulative cash inflows usable for the financing of the government budget are
      defined as external disbursements (including grants) from official multilateral creditors (including,
      but not limited to the Asian Development Bank (ADB), Islamic Development Bank, and



94    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                   PAKISTAN


      World Bank), official bilateral creditors (including, but not limited to bilateral oil facilities, China,
      Saudi Arabia, UAE, DFID-UK, and USAID), external bond placements and other commercial
      borrowings that are usable for the financing of the central government budget (including foreign
      currency financing extended by local branches of foreign banks), plus proceeds from sales of
      state-owned assets to official bilateral partners, sovereign wealth funds. Downward adjustment
      of the floor on NIR will be limited to a maximum of US$2,000 million for both the June 2026 and
      December 2026 test dates.

B.2. Ceiling on the Net Domestic Assets (NDA) of the SBP

Definition

12.    Net domestic assets of the SBP are defined as the difference between reserve money (as
defined below) minus the NIR of the SBP (as defined above). For the purposes of computing the
NDA target, the NIR is valued at the program exchange rate (¶2) and expressed in Pakistani rupee.

13.    Reserve money (RM) is defined as the sum of: (i) currency outside schedule banks (deposit
money banks); (ii) schedule banks’ domestic cash in vaults; (iii) schedule banks’ required and excess
rupee and foreign exchange deposits with the SBP; and (iv) deposits of the rest of the economy with
the SBP, excluding those held by the federal and provincial governments and the SBP staff
retirement accounts.

Adjustment Mechanism

14.   Consistent with the NIR target adjustment mechanism (as defined above), the ceiling on the
NDA will be adjusted downward (upward) by the cumulative excess (shortfall) in:

a.    cash inflows from multilateral and bilateral creditors, and commercial borrowings and
      bond issuances relative to the projected inflows (Table 1) and evaluated at the program
      exchange rate.

As in the case for NIR, the upward adjustment of the ceiling on NDA will be limited to the same
maximum adjustor limit set for NIR evaluated at the program exchange rate.

B.3. Ceiling on SBP’s Stock of Net Foreign Currency Swap/Forward Position

15.     The stock of net foreign currency swap/forward positions is defined as the aggregate
net positions in forwards and futures in foreign currencies of the SBP vis-à-vis the domestic currency
(including the forward leg of currency swaps). It will be evaluated at the program exchange rate.




                                                                     INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND         95
PAKISTAN


B.4. Ceiling on the General Government Primary Budget Deficit Excluding Grants

Definition

16.     The general government primary budget deficit (excluding grants) is monitored
quarterly as the general government’s overall budget deficit (excluding grants) minus the
consolidated interest bill of the federal and provincial budgets.

17.    The general government overall budget deficit (excluding grants) is measured as the
cash deficit from below the line, defined as the sum of:

a.   net external budget financing, excluding valuation changes, with foreign currency
     disbursements/payments converted into PRs at the actual exchange rates applied to each
     transaction;

b.   change in net domestic credit from the banking system (cash basis), excluding valuation
     changes from deposits denominated in foreign currency and government securities bought by
     non-residents (notably T-bills, Pakistan Investment bonds (PIBs), Naya Pakistan Certificates,
     and Banao Certificates);

c.   change in the net domestic nonbank financing, excluding valuation changes. These
     comprise: (i) privatization receipts (either received directly or transferred from the privatization
     accounts to the general government budget, including from abroad and in foreign currency);
     (ii) change in the stock of issued government securities held outside the general government
     and the banking system, net of valuation changes; (iii) change in net deposits and reserves
     received by the general government (public accounts deposits); (iv) any other government
     borrowing from domestic nonbank sources net of repayments; minus (v) change in general
     government deposits with nonbank financial institutions; and

d.   total external grants to the federal and provincial governments, which are defined as the
     sum of project grants, cash external grants for budgetary support, capital grants reflecting the
     principal amounts of external debt cancellation or swaps, and other grants. External grants in
     foreign currencies are converted into PRs at the actual exchange rates applied to each
     transaction.

18.    Net external budget financing (excluding valuation changes and all external financing
counted as reserve liabilities of the SBP, as defined above) is defined as the sum of:

a.   external budget loans to the general government, including those on-lent to financial
     institutions and companies (public or private) and external emergency relief lending. It
     comprises those with: (i) medium- and long-term maturity from official multilateral sources
     (including IMF budget support), official bilateral sources, and private sector sources
     (e.g., bonds and non-residents’ purchases of PIBs, Naya Pakistan Certificates, and Banao
     Certificates); and (ii) short-term maturity, net of foreign portfolio investment excluding



96   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                    PAKISTAN


     non-residents’ purchases of Naya Pakistan Certificates and Banao Certificates but including
     non-residents’ purchases of domestic T-bills; and

b.   net external debt amortization flow of the general government, which is the change in its
     stock of external debt service arrears net of the debt amortization due on its external budget
     loans (with the latter accounting for the impact of any rescheduled, relieved, or accelerated
     amortization, including that related to debt swaps or debt cancellation recorded as capital
     grants).

Adjustment Mechanism

19.    The ceiling on general government primary budget deficit (excluding grants) will be
adjusted on a cumulative basis since the beginning of the fiscal year:

a.   downward (upward) by any shortfall (excess) in external project financing relative to the
     program projections evaluated in Pakistani rupee terms at actual average quarterly exchange
     rates (see Text Table 1). External project financing is defined as disbursements from bilateral
     and multilateral creditors to the general government for specific project expenditure;

b.   downward by any under execution in the targeted cash transfers (BISP) relative to the
     indicative program target;

c.   downward by any excess in the flow of power sector payment arrears above the indicative
     program target;

d.   downward by any excess in the flow of tax refund arrears (as defined below) relative to their
     respective indicative program targets;

e.   downward by any extraordinary dividends received by the government, as per GFSM 2014
     (see ¶5.115, 5.116, 9.49), should be classified as withdrawals of equity rather than government
     revenue, This adjustor will be applicable for targets from end-September FY27 onward.

f.   downward (upward) by any excess (shortfall) in the flow of SBP profit transfer relative to their
     respective projections (Text Table 2).


                      Text Table 2. Pakistan: Projections for SBP Profit Transfer
                  (Cumulative flows from start of fiscal year; billions of Pakistani Rupees)

      end-Mar 2026               end-Jun 2026                 end-Sep 2026                 end-Dec 2026
          2,428                     2,428                         1,435                        1,435




                                                                       INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND        97
PAKISTAN


B.5. Ceiling on the Amount of Government Guarantees

20.     The ceiling on the amount of government guarantees applies to the stock of publicly
guaranteed debt for which guarantees have been issued by the central government. It includes both
domestic government guarantees, including guarantees to SOEs for commodity operations, and
external government guarantees. External government guarantees will be converted into Pakistani
rupees at the program exchange rate. If any entity incurs interest arrears on borrowings backed by
the guarantee, for purposes of the QPC such guarantee shall be evaluated as the higher of the value
of the guarantee issued or the total amount owed. This ceiling excludes guarantees issued by the
Ministry of Finance for the SBP borrowing from the IMF.

B.6. Floor on Targeted Cash Transfers Spending (BISP)

21.      The floor on targeted cash transfers spending (BISP) applies to the cumulative targeted
cash transfers on social spending by the Benazir Income Support Program (BISP), i.e., all spending on
unconditional cash transfer and conditional cash transfer BISP programs. The end-June 2026 QPC
will include spending on Q4 FY26 UCT and CCT disbursements that are delayed through end-July
2026 due to foreseen technical issues. Spending on Q4 FY26 disbursements beyond end-July 2026
will not be included in the end-June 2026 QPC or the end-September 2026 IT. All targets beyond
end-June 2026 will not allow for any delays in disbursement.

B.7. Floor on Net Tax Revenues Collected by the Federal Board of Revenue (FBR)

22.     Net tax revenues collected by the FBR are defined as the sum of revenues collected from:
(i) general sales tax (GST) on goods (including GST on services collected in Islamabad Capital
Territory); (ii) customs duties, customs registration fees and levies; (iii) excise duties on imported
products; (iv) excise duties on domestic products; (v) levies (toll) on oil derivatives; (vi) other
proceeds and fees; (vii) sales tax; and (viii) unclassified revenues (including income tax) minus the tax
refunds. Net revenue collection is defined, for each test date, as the cumulative sum of net revenues
collected since the beginning of the respective fiscal year. The floor on the collection of net
revenues by the FBR is measured quarterly based on cumulative end-of-quarter data. This will be an
indicative target for end-June 2026 and will become a QPC starting end-December 2027.

B.8. Floor on the Number of New Tax Returns

23.       The floor on the number of new income tax returns will be measured as the additional
non-nil-tax returns beyond the existing stock of non-nil-tax filers. Non-nil-tax filer returns are
defined as filers presenting a tax return with a liability greater than zero or returns that initially had a
liability of zero, which was then amended after FBR scrutiny of the return and supporting
documentation. The stock of non-nil-tax filers is 3,531,767 as of end-June 2025, and the floor on the
number of new tax returns will be counted from the starting point of 3,531,767.




98   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                              PAKISTAN


C. Continuous Performance Criteria

C.1. No New Flow of SBP’s Credit to General Government

24.     To protect the capability of the central bank to pursue its price stability objective, there
should be no new flow of SBP’s direct credit to the general government, including in the form
of purchases of public debt securities on the primary market. The performance criterion applies on
a continuous basis throughout the program period.

C.2. Zero Ceiling on the Accumulation of External Payment Arrears by the General
     Government and SOEs

25.     External payment arrears are defined as all unpaid debt-service obligations (i.e., payments
of principal and interest) of the general government (federal and provincial government, and SBP),
and state-owned enterprises to nonresidents arising in respect of public sector loans, debt
contracted or guaranteed (including unpaid penalties or interest charges associated with these
obligations that are beyond 30 days after the due date). The definition of debt, for the purposes of
the EFF, is set out in ¶8 of 2020 Guidelines on Public Debt Conditionality in Fund Arrangements. The
ceiling on the accumulation of external payment arrears is set at zero. The performance criterion
applies on a continuous basis throughout the program period.


C.3. Other Continuous Performance Criteria

26.    During the program period, Pakistan will not:

a.    impose or intensify restrictions on the making of payments and transfers for current
      international transactions;

b.    introduce or modify multiple currency practices (MCPs);

c.    conclude bilateral payment agreements that are inconsistent with Article VIII of IMF Articles
      of Agreement; and

d.    impose or intensify import restrictions for balance of payments purposes.

D. Indicative Targets

D.1. Floor on the Weighted Average Time-To-Maturity of the Domestic Debt
     Securities Stock

27.     The weighted average time-to-maturity (ATM) of the local currency domestic debt
securities stock is calculated based on the exact remaining time to maturity (in years) of each
instrument in the portfolio (T-bills, PIBs, Sukuks). The remaining time to maturity of each instrument
as of the relevant test date will be weighted by (i) the realized value at issuance for short-term



                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND      99
PAKISTAN


instruments (original maturities of one year or less), including T-bills and short-term Sukuks;
(ii) the face value for longer-term coupon-bearing instruments (original maturities greater than one
year), including PIBs and longer-term Sukuks, and (iii) nominal value (realized value plus accrued
interest) for zero-coupon bonds (both conventional instruments and Sukuks with original maturities
greater than one year).

D.2. Floor on General Government Budgetary Health and Education Spending

28.     The floor on the general government budgetary health and education spending will
apply to the cumulative budgetary spending on health and education by the federal and provincial
governments.

D.3. Ceiling on the Aggregate Provincial Primary Budget Deficit

29.     The aggregate provincial primary budget deficit is monitored quarterly as the aggregate
overall budget deficit of the four provincial governments (Punjab, Sindh, Khyber Pakhtunkhaw,
Balochistan), minus the aggregate interest bill of the four provincial budgets.

30.    The aggregate provincial overall budget deficit is measured as the cash deficit from
below the line.

D.4. Floor on the Consolidated Net Tax Revenues Collected by Provinces

31.      Net tax revenues collected by the provinces are defined as the sum of revenues collected
from: (i) general sales tax (GST) on services (excluding GST on services collected in Islamabad Capital
Territory); (ii) stamp duties; (iii) property tax; (iv) agriculture income tax; (v) registration fees; and
(vi) unclassified revenues minus the tax refunds. Net revenue collection is defined, for each test date,
as the cumulative sum of net revenues collected since the beginning of the respective fiscal year.
The floor on the collection of net revenues by the provinces is measured quarterly based on
cumulative end-of-quarter data.

D.5. Ceiling on Net Accumulation of Tax Refund Arrears

32.      The ceiling on the net accumulation of tax refund arrears applies to the cumulative flow
of tax refund arrears. The stock of tax refund arrears is defined as the amount of tax refund claims
supported by requisite documentation that have not been settled (through a cash refund, netting
out against obligations of taxpayers, payment with a government bond/promissory note or an
official decision to reject the claim) within a specified time period after the tax refund claim has been
submitted to the FBR. The stock of income tax refund arrears is PRs 234.8 billion as of end-June
2025, and the net accumulation of income tax refund arrears will be counted from the starting point
of PRs 234.8 billion; the stock of sales tax arrears at end-June 2025 is PRs 155.5 billion, and the net
accumulation of sales tax refund arrears will be counted from the starting point of PRs 155.5 billion.




100 INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                                    PAKISTAN


D.6. Floor on the Income Tax Revenue collected by the FBR from Retailers

33.      The floor on the income tax revenue from retailers is defined as the sum of revenues
from: (i) income tax return filings; (ii) withholding income taxes; and (iii) advance income taxes, paid
by (i) retailers; (ii) wholesalers; and (iii) traders. Income tax revenue is defined, for each test date, as
the cumulative sum of revenues collected since the beginning of the respective fiscal year.

D.7. Ceiling on Power Sector Payment Arrears

34.      Power sector payment arrears are defined as power sector payables in arrears that arise
from: (i) line losses and non-recoveries that are not recognized by NEPRA; (ii) non-recoveries from
supply to Azad Jammu and Kashmir (AJ&K), other federal and provincial governments including
FATA, private consumers, and Baluchistan Tube Wells; (iii) accrued markup from the servicing of
PHPL; (iv) late payment surcharges; (v) delays in subsidy payments; (vi) delays in tariff determinations
resulting in pending generation cost; (vii) non-payments by K-Electric; (viii) loan principal
repayments and net debt service surcharges; and (ix) other adjustments (including prior year
recoveries, spillovers etc.).


E. Key Performance Indicators for Priority Areas of FBR’s Transformation
Plan

35.     FBR will provide monthly information on the outturns for key performance indicators
(see MEFP ¶9 a b and c). The authorities will set specific targets for the following performance
indicators in consultation with the IMF staff at the time of FY27 budget preparation:
• The value of audit adjustments (excl. penalties and interest) paid within 120 days of audit
    completion (Prs Bn);
• Completed audit cases with adjustment paid within 120 days, as a percentage of all cases
    completed (percent);
• The total value of digital invoices processed by the invoicing system;
• The number of active sales tax payers using the system;
• The increase in sales tax revenues from monitored locations;
• The volume of goods identified in monitored locations.

PAKISTAN

needed data to the IMF in line with Article VIII, Section 5 of the IMF Articles of Agreement as
deemed necessary. Performance under the program is monitored from data supplied to the IMF by
the SBP, Ministry of Finance, FBR, Pakistan Bureau of Statistics, Ministry of Energy (Power and
Petroleum Division), and other agencies as outlined in Table 2 below. Irrespective of the
requirements outlined in Table 2, the authorities will report on an ongoing/continuous basis any
non-observance of continuous PCs. The authorities will transmit promptly to IMF staff any data
revisions as well as other information necessary to monitor the arrangement with the IMF.

                 THIRD REVIEW UNDER THE EXTENDED ARRANGEMENT
April 23, 2026
                 UNDER THE EXTENDED FUND FACILITY, SECOND REVIEW
                 UNDER THE RESILIENCE AND SUSTAINABILITY FACILITY
                 ARRANGEMENT—WORLD BANK ASSESSMENT LETTER FOR
                 THE RESILIENCE AND SUSTAINABILITY FACILITY
                 This note provides the World Bank Group’s current assessment of Pakistan’s climate
                 vulnerability, and of government policies and commitments on adaptation and mitigation,
                 focusing on material developments since the previous assessment letter (May 7, 2025). It
                 also summarizes WBG engagement relevant to Pakistan’s RSF-supported program.

                 COUNTRY VULNERABILITY TO CLIMATE CHANGE,
                 INCLUDING HUMAN, SOCIAL AND ECONOMIC COSTS

                 1.     Pakistan remains highly exposed to climate shocks, including recurrent
                 floods, intensifying heatwaves, and accelerated glacial melt, with implications for
                 macro fiscal stability, livelihoods, and poverty reduction. In 2025, severe monsoon
                 floods caused multibillion dollar damages and widespread displacement, echoing the
                 catastrophic 2022 floods and underscoring persistent systemic risk. Recent Climate Risk
                 Index assessments also reflect Pakistan’s elevated vulnerability over both single year
                 and long-term horizons.


                 A. Government Policies and Commitments in Terms of Climate
                 Change Adaptation and Priority Areas to Strengthen Resilience

                 2.     There is sustained progress towards the implementation of National
                 Adaptation Plan (NAP): Following the launch of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) in
                 2023, the Ministry of Climate Change & Environmental Coordination (MoCC&EC) is
                 developing a digital monitoring and evaluation system for the NAP, which is expected
                 to be completed by mid-2026. This system will allow the Government, for the first time,
                 to systematically track sectoral and provincial adaptation progress, strengthen
                 transparency of implementation, and provide a platform for integrating climate risk data
                 into broader planning processes.
PAKISTAN




3.      The Prime Minister has also endorsed a national flood preparedness plan in light
of the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) warnings that the that the 2026
monsoon season may be more intense, with projections for increased rainfall severity and
geographic spread. The plan focuses on improving federal provincial coordination on disaster
response; strengthening early warning systems; prioritizing flood mitigation infrastructure; and
restoring or reinforcing climate damage assets. In parallel, NDMA issued the National Disaster
Response Plan 2026, which provides a comprehensive and scalable framework to guide
response operations across government tiers. Furthermore, the MoCC&EC is drafting a National
Glacier Preservation Strategy, currently under consultation with federal and provincial
stakeholders. The Strategy is intended to fill a gap in systematic glacier monitoring and to
support integration of glacier related data into national and provincial water planning,
recognizing Pakistan’s unique vulnerability as home to the world’s largest concentration of
nonpolar glaciers.

4.       2026 monsoon season may be more intense, with projections for increased rainfall
severity and geographic spread. The plan focuses on improving federal provincial
coordination on disaster response; strengthening early warning systems; prioritizing flood
mitigation infrastructure; and restoring or reinforcing climate damage assets. In parallel, NDMA
issued the National Disaster Response Plan 2026, which provides a comprehensive and scalable
framework to guide response operations across government tiers. Furthermore, the MoCC&EC
is drafting a National Glacier Preservation Strategy, currently under consultation with federal
and provincial stakeholders. The Strategy is intended to fill a gap in systematic glacier
monitoring and to support integration of glacier related data into national and provincial water
planning, recognizing Pakistan’s unique vulnerability as home to the world’s largest
concentration of nonpolar glaciers.

5.       As part of the RSF program, Pakistan has introduced climate risk assessments for
all public investment projects exceeding PKR 7.5 billion, mandating all large
infrastructure proposals systematically evaluate exposure to floods, heatwaves, and other
climate hazards before approval. This is the first time Pakistan has moved toward integrating
climate risk screening into its core development programming processes. In parallel, the
Government has adopted minimum climate related expenditure shares, requiring at least 30%
of total infrastructure spending to be directed toward climate relevant or climate resilient
measures, reflecting an effort to reorient public investment toward adaptation and resilience.

6.       Climate budget tagging continues to expand. At the federal level, ministries are
required to classify budget lines based on their climate relevance, and provinces—especially
Punjab—have begun operationalizing climate budget tagging at scale, embedding it into
annual development planning. Punjab’s FY2025–26 Annual Development Programme tagged
Rs795 billion as climate related (including Rs277 billion for adaptation investments across
water, agriculture, and urban sectors). This represents a substantial shift toward subnational
integration of climate priorities into routine public financial management.



2    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                     PAKISTAN




7.      Financial regulators have taken concrete steps to align the financial system with
Pakistan’s climate resilience objectives. On December 10, 2025, the State Bank of Pakistan
issued a circular requiring all banks and Development Finance Institutions to adopt the Pakistan
Green Taxonomy, ensuring that lending and investment portfolios incorporate nationally
defined green criteria beginning in 2026. Simultaneously, the Securities and Exchange
Commission of Pakistan issued revised ESG Disclosure Guidelines for listed companies. These
guidelines introduce a phased timetable under which climate risk disclosures become
mandatory by June 2029, strengthening transparency and supporting the transition to climate-
aligned capital flows. On May 16, 2025, Pakistan launched its first Sovereign Domestic Green
Sukuk valued at US$100m at the Pakistan Stock Exchange, to fund climate-resilient and new
energy projects in the country.

8.      The State of Bank of Pakistan has also launched a climate stress testing
framework, requiring phased implementation by June 2029. The framework promotes
systematic assessment of banks’ exposure to climate related transition and physical risks,
helping strengthen the financial sector’s resilience and supporting climate aligned credit
allocation over time. Together, these measures represent a significant institutional step toward
integrating climate considerations across Pakistan’s financial system.


 B. Government Policies and Commitments in Terms of Climate Change
 Mitigation and Priority Areas to Reduce Greenhouse Gas Emissions

9.      Pakistan’s energy transition has continued to gain momentum, with renewables
surpassing earlier national targets ahead of schedule. Strong solar investments—especially
several gigawatts of distributed generation capacity by consumers — has positioned solar as
the fastest‑growing contributor to the power mix. Subnational initiatives, particularly in Punjab
and Sindh, have accelerated the uptake of distributed solar, including through public facilities,
municipal programs, and rooftop adoption reforms.


 10.    At the federal level, the regulatory landscape evolved with the introduction of the
 Prosumer Regulations 2026, shifting new applicants from net metering to a net billing
 framework. While this reform aims to ensure long‑term financial sustainability of the power
 system, it has introduced new dynamics for private investment decisions by altering payback
 periods for households and commercial users. Ensuring predictable and well‑sequenced policy
 signals will be important to maintain investor confidence and continue scaling renewables at
 the pace required to meet Pakistan’s medium‑term clean energy objectives.


 11.    The New Energy Vehicle policy 2025–2030 represents a significant step toward
 decarbonizing Pakistan’s transport sector. It establishes phased national targets for electric
 vehicle (EV) penetration across two‑wheelers, three‑wheelers, and passenger vehicles; provides
 calibrated purchase subsidies; and outlines fiscal incentives to support domestic assembly and



                                                             INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND        3
PAKISTAN




charging‑infrastructure growth. The policy also mandates the future electrification of portions
of the federal public fleet, reflecting the government’s commitment to lead by example. A
notable feature of this new policy is its explicit inclusion of social equity measures, reserving a
portion of EV subsidies for women and lower‑income consumers. This reflects an effort to
ensure that the transition to clean mobility supports both environmental goals and social
inclusion priorities.


12.    Pakistan has continued to strengthen its readiness for international market‑based
mitigation under Article 6.2 of the Paris Agreement. The MoCC&EC has recently issued
Letters of Intent for several mitigation activities, including two World Bank‑supported projects:
the Punjab Rural Sustainable Water Supply & Sanitation Project and the Sindh Solid Waste
Emergency and Efficiency Project. These engagements are expected to generate verified
emissions reductions and Internationally Transferred Mitigation Outcomes, unlocking
approximately US$40 million in results‑based climate finance once verification milestones are
met. This signals initial steps towards realizing a significant carbon finance potential if
regulatory frameworks and institutional coordination are further strengthened.


13.     The Government has also advanced fiscal reforms to support decarbonization,
most notably by institutionalizing a carbon levy with a legislated escalation path. The
levy is intended to discourage emissions‑intensive fuels, encourage EV adoption, and reduce
the economy’s reliance on fossil energy. It is also projected to generate meaningful fiscal
revenues that could support broader climate priorities. A clearer framework for monitoring the
allocation and climate relevance of levy proceeds would further strengthen transparency, help
demonstrate the instrument’s mitigation contribution, and reinforce its alignment with
Pakistan’s national climate objectives.


C. Other Challenges and Opportunities

14.      Pakistan’s energy sector continues to navigate significant macro‑fiscal pressures
as the government implements tariff rationalization and subsidy reforms. Circular debt—
estimated at Rs 1.764 trillion in the power sector and Rs 3.442 trillion in the gas sector as of
early 2026—has led the Government’s commitments under the IMF program to implement
regular tariff adjustments, phase out untargeted subsidies, convert the accumulated stock in
the power sector into CPPA-G liabilities and introducing an extra charge for electricity
consumers to retire the principle stock amount. While these reforms aim to restore financial
sustainability, they also create short‑term affordability challenges. Looking forward, annual
electricity tariff rebasing, semi‑annual gas tariff adjustments, and the planned introduction of a
carbon levy beginning July 2025 are expected to gradually align price signals with underlying
costs, which could help crowd in private investment and create fiscal space for climate‑related
spending, provided reforms are implemented predictably and accompanied by safeguards,
while targeting for vulnerable consumers could be further improved.



4    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                             PAKISTAN




15.     Ensuring stable and predictable policy signals remains essential for sustaining
private‑sector participation in Pakistan’s clean energy transition. Recent regulatory
adjustments—such as the shift in prosumer rules from net metering to net billing, along with
selected changes in transport‑related taxation affecting hybrid and electric vehicles—have
introduced new dynamics for investors and households. While these reforms aim to align
incentives with long‑term system sustainability, the pace and sequence of policy changes
underscore the importance of clear communication, regulatory continuity, and transitional
arrangements. Strengthening predictability in the investment environment, alongside careful
attention to distributional impacts, will help maintain public confidence and support the
expansion of clean technologies across all income groups.

16.     Pakistan continues to advance its disaster risk financing agenda, with steps
underway to develop a national DRF strategy that can improve the timeliness and
predictability of post‑disaster resource flows. Building on existing instruments, the
emerging strategy seeks to create an integrated framework that combines ex‑ante financial
preparedness tools—such as contingency financing, risk layering, and insurance mechanisms—
with more traditional ex‑post budgetary responses. Operationalizing these instruments will
require clear institutional mandates, strengthened coordination between federal and provincial
authorities, and improved data systems to support risk modelling and payout triggers. As
progress continues, further formalization of financing instruments will help ensure that
resources can be mobilized rapidly and transparently in response to increasingly frequent
climate‑related shocks.

17.      The development of an economy‑wide long‑term Low‑emissions Strategy (LTS)
represents an important next step in consolidating Pakistan’s climate mitigation vision
beyond the time‑bound objectives of its NDCs.1 An LTS would help articulate sectoral
transition pathways, clarify sequencing of reforms, and provide long‑term signals to investors
on the direction of Pakistan’s energy, transport, agriculture, and industrial transformation. Such
a strategy would also strengthen the coherence of policy frameworks across ministries,
support the prioritization of climate‑aligned public investment, and reinforce Pakistan’s
credibility in accessing international climate finance. Advancing work on the LTS would
therefore complement existing policies and deepen the institutional architecture needed for
sustained emissions reductions.


18.    Pakistan’s growing exposure to climate‑linked trade measures, particularly the
EU’s evolving Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM), underscores the
importance of accelerating decarbonization within key export sectors. While current


1
  Under Article 4.19 of the Paris Agreement, all countries are encouraged to formulate and communicate long-
term low greenhouse gas emission development strategies to the UNFCCC



                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND            5
PAKISTAN




CBAM coverage is limited, potential expansion to additional products—such as textiles—could
create new competitiveness challenges unless firms are equipped to measure, report, and
verify emissions at the facility level. Preparing for this emerging landscape will require
enhanced MRV readiness, sector‑specific decarbonization pathways, and strengthened
collaboration between environmental and trade authorities. Proactive measures taken now can
help preserve market access, support export resilience, and position Pakistan to benefit from
the global shift toward lower‑carbon value chains.

D. World Bank Group Engagement Related to Climate Change

19.     The World Bank Group's 10-year Country Partnership Framework (2026–35)
provides the overarching structure for its climate engagement in Pakistan. Committing up
to US$20 billion in World Bank lending and a further US$20 billion in IFC and MIGA private
sector mobilization — a total envelope of US$40 billion at US$1.5–2 billion per year. Two of
the six CPF outcomes are explicitly climate-focused: Outcome 3 (Increased resilience to climate
change) and Outcome 4 (Cleaner energy and better air quality). Since FY2014, all IDA/IBRD
projects in Pakistan have been screened for climate and disaster risks, with 39% of
commitments delivering climate co-benefits across adaptation and mitigation.

The WBG portfolio includes both on-going operations and a strong pipeline of operations
under preparation, supported by strong knowledge and analytical program is leading
transformative engagements in clean air, flood resilient reconstruction, carbon market
readiness, urban resilience, grid capacity for renewable integration, and reduce air pollution.
Select examples include:

• Under Implementation:
    o   Punjab Clean Air Program: In the context of improving air quality, the program
        develops a comprehensive assessment to support the Punjab Industry sector towards
        the transition to resource efficiency and cleaner production with a focus on clean
        energy.

    o   Pakistan Public Resources for Inclusive Development MPA – Federal (PRID-
        MPA): Strengthens Pakistan's federal fiscal system to support macroeconomic stability
        and service delivery through improvements in revenue collection, public expenditure
        efficiency, and data systems. Of direct climate relevance, the program has a
        component on rationalization of energy subsidies, supporting the transition away from
        broad, policy-distortive electricity subsidies toward a targeted welfare-based model —
        reducing fiscal drag from the power sector while improving incentives for clean energy
        investment.

    o   Punjab Inclusive Cities Program (PICP): Enhances urban resilience and climate-proof
        service delivery across 16 secondary cities in Punjab, aligned with the NAP's urban




6    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND
                                                                                      PAKISTAN




        resilience pillar. This includes 2 Public Private Partnership water projects via IFC PPP
        transaction advisory.

    o   Sindh Flood Emergency Housing Reconstruction Project (SFEHRP): Supports
        beneficiary-driven, multi-hazard resilient reconstruction of core housing units
        damaged or destroyed by the catastrophic 2022 floods across 24 affected districts of
        Sindh. Through owner-driven cash grants, skills development on climate resilient
        construction practices, and technical assistance to the Government of Sindh, the
        project is facilitating the reconstruction of approximately 770,000 houses by 2028.

•   Under Preparation:
    o   Boosting Energy Security through Transmission in Pakistan (BEST-PAK) MPA
        Program Phase 1: A 10-year program aims to modernize Pakistan's national
        transmission infrastructure to improve grid reliability and enhance capacity to absorb
        cleaner energy resources at scale while ensure improving system resilience and overall
        security of electricity supply, directly supporting NDC 3.0's clean energy targets.

    o   Pakistan Finance for Lowering Emissions in Water and Waste Sectors (Pak-FLOW)
        Project: Will develop a MRV systems needed to support Pakistan's access an
        approximate $40 million in climate finance under Article 6 of the Paris Agreement,
        including the recently issued Letter of Intents to 2 WB-funded projects (noted above).

    o   Resilience, Early Action, and Disaster Readiness for KP (READY-KP): Strengthens
        flood and disaster resilience in one of Pakistan's most climate-exposed provinces
        through resilient infrastructure, an integrated Flood Early Warning System, and
        institutional preparedness capacity building targeting 2.5 million direct beneficiaries.

    o   Khyber Pakhtunkhwa Clean Air Project: Aims to strengthen air quality management
        and reduce population-weighted PM2.5 annual average exposure in the Peshawar
        Basin in Kyber Pakhtunkhwa Province with expected financing of US $200mn.

20.     IFC is actively supporting Pakistan’s climate and sustainability transition through
a broad set of advisory and financing initiatives. Its work includes helping the State Bank of
Pakistan develop green banking guidelines and an Environmental & Social Risk Management
manual, development of the Pakistan Green Taxonomy (in collaboration with WB), and
pioneering Pakistan’s first sustainability linked loans with gender and water KPIs, with
additional Sustainability Linked Loan and Green Bond issuances in the pipeline. IFC also
provides energy efficiency advisory, and green building certification through the EDGE
program—while conducting ESG advisory to strengthen environmental and social risk practices
in the banking sector and capital markets. IFC is rolling out an e-mobility advisory with the
Ministry of Production and Industries. This project is designed to strengthen policies,
regulations, and standards to incentivize investment in local manufacturing of e-2/3Ws. It will
help to accelerate the adoption of e-2/3Ws in Pakistan through developing quality safety



                                                              INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND          7
PAKISTAN




testing standards and regulatory frameworks for manufacturing and charging infrastructure
and vehicles as well as the removal of administrative barriers that inhibit investment. To scale
climate finance, IFC is implementing the US$120 million CIFPAK blended finance facility,
the largest single country program of its kind by IFC or the UK, aimed at creating an enabling
ecosystem for climate investment and an adaptation focused project pipeline.

21.     MIGA engagement in Pakistan focuses on mobilizing private capital for clean
energy and improving the enabling environment for investment. MIGA currently provides
US$82.7 million in guarantees supporting the 102‑MW Gulpur run‑of‑river hydropower
project, contributing to low‑carbon generation capacity and reducing reliance on
higher‑emissions thermal power. In addition, MIGA is working with the Government of
Pakistan and financial sector stakeholders to strengthen the investment climate and expand
the role of political risk insurance and credit enhancement instruments—support that
complements IFC’s private‑sector development initiatives.

22.      Pakistan has advanced a number of RSF‑relevant reforms and systems since the
last Assessment Letter. Notably in adaptation planning and monitoring, climate‑responsive
public financial management, financial‑sector alignment, renewable energy deployment, clean
transport policy, and Article 6 readiness. Continued progress would be further strengthened
by: (i) maintaining policy predictability to support private investment; (ii) formalizing DRF
instruments; (iii) articulating an economy‑wide long‑term strategy; and (iv) accelerating
CBAM‑related readiness. The World Bank Group stands ready to continue supporting Pakistan
in implementing a credible, well‑sequenced, and inclusive climate transition consistent with
RSF objectives.

              This supplement provides an update on the status of the outstanding prior actions (PAs), and
              on program performance since the issuance of the report on April 24. These recent
              developments do not alter the thrust of the staff appraisal.
              1.      The authorities met the two outstanding prior actions:
              •    The weekly fuel price adjustment on April 24, 2026, restored full alignment of
                   domestic fuel prices with international prices, and this was maintained in the weekly
                   adjustment on May 1, 2026. Given the surge in international crack spreads on high-
                   speed diesel (HSD), the diesel pricing formula has been adjusted for a 3-month period
                   to reference the cost of domestically-refined HSD, with an additional premium added
                   to ensure full cost recovery on imported diesel.

              •    As of April 30, 2026, FBR has collected PRs 327.7 billion (0.3 percent of GDP) from
                   disputed taxpayer obligations meeting the prior action’s target of PRs 322 billion. As
                   anticipated, most of this amount (PRs 318.4 billion) was based on a recent court ruling
                   confirming the legality of the super tax. As also required under the prior action, on
                   April 21st, 2026, the Finance Secretary issued a decision reducing the FY26 expenditure
                   ceilings by PRs 136 billion (0.1 percent of GDP).

              2.        Most end-March Indicative Targets (ITs) for which data is available have
              been met (see Table 1). The authorities met the ITs on (i) the floor on the net
              international reserves of the SBP; (ii) the ceiling on the net domestic assets of the SBP;
              (iii) the ceiling on the SBP’s stock of net foreign currency swaps/forward position; and
              (iv) the ceiling on net accumulation of tax refund arrears. The authorities also met the
              continuous PCs on the zero new flow of SBP’s credit to the general government and the
              zero ceiling on accumulation of external public payments arrears of the general
PAKISTAN




government. FBR net revenue collection reached PRs 9.306 trillion against the Second Review
target of 9.917 trillion – a shortfall of 0.5 percent of GDP. Power sector payment arrears were PRs
420 billion through end-March, against a target of PRs 400 billion; this was driven by non-
operational arrears that remain to be settled, despite continued strong performance on
collections and losses.

3.      Amid rising inflation pressures, the SBP increased the main policy rate by 100 basis
points at its April 27 meeting. CPI inflation increased to 10.9 percent (yoy) in April, driven by a
combination of higher fuel prices and adverse food price base effects, broadly in line with the staff
report projections. Core inflation increased to 8.2 percent (yoy) in April, driven by the pass-
through of higher fuel costs to transport service prices. According to the SBP, the tightening of
the policy rate was aimed at containing emerging inflationary pressures and limiting second
round effects from higher energy prices.
