4
          Statement by Mr. Bahador Bijani and Mr. Saif Ullah Dogar on Pakistan
                                     May 8, 2026




On behalf of the Pakistani authorities, we convey our sincere appreciation to the Fund staff and
Management for their sustained engagement and constructive policy dialogue under the Extended
Fund Facility (EFF) and the Resilience and Sustainability Facility (RSF). The program remains
the central policy anchor for restoring macroeconomic stability, rebuilding external and fiscal
buffers, and advancing structural reforms in a highly uncertain external environment. The
authorities reaffirm their strong commitment to the objectives of both arrangements and to the
policy framework set out in the Letter of Intent. Despite renewed pressures from global commodity
price volatility, they have maintained firm ownership and continue to implement agreed measures
in a timely and transparent manner. The authorities sincerely appreciate the unwavering trust and
financial assistance provided by their bilateral and multilateral partners, especially the People's
Republic of China and the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia.

  I.    Program Performance

Program implementation has remained on track, reflecting firm political ownership and
institutional commitment. All seven quantitative performance criteria at end-December 2025 were
met, including an overperformance on the net international reserves floor, zero accumulation of
external payment arrears, and the ceiling on the general government primary deficit—underscoring
disciplined macroeconomic management and improved policy coordination. Six of eight indicative
targets were also met. Where shortfalls arose in Federal Board of Revenue (FBR) tax revenues and
retailer income tax collection, they were diagnostic rather than systemic, reflecting sector-specific
weaknesses in power, oil, and gas-related collections. Corrective actions are embedded in the
program, including the proposed upgrading of the FBR revenue floor to a quantitative performance
criterion from end-December 2026.

Five of six structural benchmarks were met, covering tax administration, governance, social
support, energy sector sustainability, and investment zone reform. The one missed benchmark—
on Sovereign Wealth Fund law amendments—reflected the need for extensive technical
consultations; the draft amendments have been finalized and will shortly be submitted to
Parliament. The continuous SB on preferential tax treatment was briefly missed due to an extension
of the sugar import exemption, which was subsequently repealed with no utilization. Both RSF
Reform Measures due at this review (RM7 on climate-related financial risk guidelines and RM8
on climate-related corporate disclosures) were implemented in December 2025. Taken together,
performance under the EFF and RSF demonstrates that the program is functioning as a credible
policy anchor rather than a narrow compliance exercise.
                                                 2


 II.    Macroeconomic Developments and Risks

Macroeconomic stabilization has continued to consolidate. Output growth averaged 3.8 percent
year-on-year in FY26H1, inflation remained contained prior to the recent commodity shock, and
gross reserves exceeded program targets—surpassing US$16 billion at end-December 2025. The
exchange rate has remained broadly stable, supported by proactive reserve accumulation and
restored confidence.

The resurgence of geopolitical tensions in the Middle East has, however, materially intensified
global uncertainty and introduced new downside risks through higher energy prices, tighter
financial conditions, and pressures on remittances and capital flows. Pakistan's significant reliance
on Gulf energy supplies and remittances makes it particularly exposed to these developments.
While the external position has remained broadly stable—supported by sustained remittance
inflows and contained import demand—it remains sensitive to oil price shocks, which could affect
both the current account and inflation dynamics.

Under the updated baseline, GDP growth in FY26 is projected at 3.6 percent, with inflation
expected to temporarily exceed 10 percent in FY26 before returning to the SBP's target range by
FY28. The authorities' response is firmly anchored in program principles—allowing prices and the
exchange rate to adjust, maintaining fiscal discipline, and deploying only targeted and budget-
neutral support for vulnerable households—while preserving the flexibility to recalibrate as
conditions evolve.

The authorities have carefully noted the scenario analysis presented by staff on the potential
macroeconomic impact of a protracted regional war, including under adverse conditions. They
consider this assessment a valuable analytical foundation and remain fully engaged with staff to
develop and, if necessary, operationalize an appropriate and effective policy response should
downside risks materialize. The authorities are committed to maintaining an open and proactive
policy dialogue with the Fund to ensure that any significant deviation from the baseline is addressed
swiftly, in a manner consistent with program objectives and the broader macroeconomic stability
framework.

III.   Fiscal Policy, Revenue Mobilization, and Public Financial Management

The authorities reaffirm their commitment to the FY26 underlying primary balance target of 1.6
percent of GDP. The primary surplus of 3.2 percent of GDP recorded in FY26H1 comfortably
exceeded the program target of 2.5 percent of GDP, notwithstanding an FBR revenue shortfall that
was offset by higher petroleum development levy collections, strong provincial revenues, and
lower flood response costs. As prior actions, the authorities have committed to collecting 0.3
percent of GDP in overdue tax revenues following favorable court rulings, and to formalizing FY26
expenditure savings from unutilized emergency allocations. Domestic fuel prices have been fully
realigned with international markets in line with program commitments. This sustained fiscal
                                                  3


consolidation has played a pivotal role in reducing macroeconomic imbalances, easing demand-
side pressures, and supporting both disinflation and external account stabilization.

The FY27 budget is being formulated to deliver an underlying primary surplus of 2.0 percent of
GDP. Permanent revenue measures of 0.3 percent of GDP are anchored in the FBR Transformation
Plan—encompassing risk-based audits, mandatory digital invoicing, and sectoral production
monitoring—alongside streamlining of tax expenditures and further expansion of the provincial
GST on services base. Primary current spending growth will be capped at the projected inflation
rate, while fiscal space is explicitly reallocated toward social protection, health, and education.

These efforts reflect a deliberate shift toward more durable, efficiency-enhancing revenue
mobilization and expenditure prioritization, aimed at creating space for growth-supporting and pro-
poor spending while preserving fiscal discipline.

Public financial management reforms—including TSA expansion, climate-weighted PSDP
appraisal, procurement transparency through e-PADS, and PPP fiscal risk monitoring—continue to
reinforce institutional credibility. Together, these reforms are strengthening expenditure control,
improving allocative efficiency, and enhancing fiscal transparency—key pillars for sustaining
market confidence and policy credibility.

IV.     Energy Pricing and Energy Sector Reforms

The authorities have maintained full cost-recovery passthrough of international energy prices
through regular fuel, power, and gas tariff adjustments. Any temporary relief measures adopted in
the context of exceptional commodity price volatility remain explicitly financed, limited in
duration, targeted to vulnerable households, and budget-neutral—consistent with the Letter of
Intent and without compromising the sector's pricing framework.

In the power sector, improved DISCO operational performance and stronger enforcement measures
supported the achievement of the end-December 2025 circular debt flow target, allowing the FY27
circular debt flow target to be set 25 percent lower than that of FY26. The authorities are finalizing
IPP penalty payment settlements and advancing toward a competitive electricity market. DISCO
private sector participation, transmission restructuring, and the first wheeling auction are
progressing in line with the structural reform agenda. These reforms are expected to enhance
efficiency, reduce losses, and gradually lower the structural cost of energy supply, thereby
improving competitiveness and easing medium-term fiscal risks.

In the gas sector, semi-annual tariff adjustments aligned with cost recovery are being maintained.
A comprehensive, audited gas circular debt dataset has been finalized and is being disseminated
quarterly—a critical step toward a viable FY27 CD Management Plan. The National Integrated
Energy Plan Secretariat is improving supply-demand coordination across the sector. Improved data
transparency and coordinated planning are laying the groundwork for a more sustainable and
predictable energy sector framework.
                                                 4


 V.     Social Protection and Human Capital

Protecting the most vulnerable remains a binding program commitment. Social spending floors
were met at end-December 2025, and minor execution delays at the subnational level reflected
administrative capacity constraints rather than any dilution of policy intent. The Kafaalat inflation
adjustment was implemented on schedule. The FY27 BISP budget will increase quarterly UCT
benefit levels by ~24 percent beginning January 2027, moving meaningfully toward the authorities'
objective of quarterly benefits equivalent to 15 percent of the lowest income quintile's family
consumption basket. Health and education spending rose by 0.3 percent of GDP in FY26 and is
targeted to increase by a further 0.2 percent of GDP in FY27. The ongoing transition to digital
delivery through Social Protection Wallets is improving transparency and payment efficiency.
Digitalization is also expected to strengthen targeting accuracy, reduce leakages, and enhance the
overall effectiveness of social spending.

VI.     Monetary Policy, Exchange Rate, and Financial Sector Stability

Monetary policy continues to prioritize price stability and the durable anchoring of inflation
expectations. Following measured easing in December 2025, the Monetary Policy Committee
prudently held the policy rate at 10.5 percent in the subsequent meetings given renewed
commodity-driven inflation risks. The SBP's commitment to price stability has been reinforced by
concrete policy action. At its most recent Monetary Policy Committee meeting on April 27, 2026,
the SBP raised its benchmark policy rate by 100 basis points to 11.5 percent—the first rate increase
since June 2023—amid rising inflationary pressures and heightened global uncertainty. The
decision was driven by the need to anchor inflation expectations and contain second-round effects
stemming from supply-side shocks, including elevated energy costs, currency pressures, and
volatile global oil prices linked to geopolitical developments. Notably, headline inflation had
accelerated to 7.3 percent in March 2026—the highest since August 2024—breaching the SBP's 5–
7 percent target range for the first time since October 2024. In this context, the SBP stands ready
to tighten policy further as needed to prevent second-round effects from becoming entrenched,
while continuing reforms to strengthen policy communication and the monetary transmission
mechanism.

Exchange rate flexibility remains the primary shock absorber. The interbank market continues to
function effectively, and the SBP is preparing a roadmap for gradual, phased, and conditionsbased
FX market liberalization. In parallel, the authorities are taking deliberate steps to strengthen the
external financing architecture and broaden the investor base. Plans to access the Panda bond
market reflect a considered strategy to diversify external financing sources, extend the maturity
profile of sovereign liabilities, and affirm Pakistan's commitment to debt sustainability and market
credibility—complementing the recent successful return to the Eurobond market and the broader
effort to rebuild the country's international financial standing. Financial sector stability is being
safeguarded through proactive supervision: the previously undercapitalized private bank has met
capital requirements, and decisive actions are underway to address vulnerabilities in the
microfinance banking sector. A post-2027 financial sector strategy, the Virtual Assets regulatory
                                                 5


framework, and continued AML/CFT reforms—particularly on beneficial ownership transparency
and trade-based money laundering mitigation—further reinforce systemic resilience.

VII.    Governance, SOE Reforms, and Business Environment

The Economic Governance Reform plan, published at end-December 2025, provides a monitorable
framework for delivering on the Governance Diagnostic Assessment's priority recommendations,
with semi-annual progress reports and institutionalized stakeholder engagement. SOE governance
reforms are advancing through legal harmonization with the SOE Act, full operationalization of the
Central Monitoring Unit, IFRS-compliant financial reporting, and the incorporation of public
service obligation frameworks into the FY27 budget. SWF law amendments—clarifying its
mandate and strengthening fiscal safeguards—are pending parliamentary approval, with the SWF's
operationalization contingent on their enactment. The privatization agenda is gaining momentum,
led by the PIA agreement and advancing DISCO private sector participation. Business climate
reforms through the National Tariff Policy, removal of non-tariff barriers, phase-out of special zone
fiscal incentives by 2035, and the Asaan Karobar regulatory streamlining framework are
progressively eliminating structural distortions and improving the investment environment.

VIII.   Climate Agenda under the RSF

Both RSF Reform Measures due at this review were met in December 2025, strengthening
Pakistan's climate information architecture and advancing the management of climate-related
financial risks within the financial sector. Progress toward forthcoming reform measures is
ongoing, covering disaster risk financing coordination, climate-weighted public investment
appraisal, and the transition to a targeted electricity subsidy framework for low-income consumers
linked to the BISP registry. These reforms are aligned with the program's objective of reducing
climate-related balance-of-payments vulnerabilities while enhancing long-term macroeconomic
resilience.

 Conclusion

The Pakistani authorities remain fully committed to the programs’ objectives. Strong performance
at end-December 2025, combined with credible policy commitments and a principled response to
the Middle East commodity shock, demonstrates the resilience of the policy framework and the
depth of institutional ownership. The combination of disciplined macroeconomic policies, concrete
structural reforms, and targeted social protection provides a credible pathway toward durable
stability, inclusive growth, and enhanced resilience. The authorities reaffirm their determination to
sustain reform momentum and navigate heightened external risks in close partnership with the Fund
and development partners.
